› Message from general-purpose
  ## Verdict
  Your lead holds. The new step data is true to git, and so is the product code:
  it applies today's configuration exactly as written. What is wrong is this
  repository's `.live-docs.config.json`. When the archive moved on November 18
  (`c6da091e`), its `bundleExclude` patterns stopped naming the archive's old
  layout, so the history no longer leaves out records at old commits. The case's
  expectation (written November 11, `da202041`, when the patterns named
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/**`) matches what the configuration means. No
  mend avoids a rebuild cleanly, so I built nothing. I made no commits; the
  branch is still at `892833fc`, and I deleted my copy of the cache.

  ## Cause, shown
  - **Where links are filtered.** Doc links are filtered once, when a commit is
  summarized: `summarizeChange.ts` calls `relatedDocsOf(file, graph,
  configuration.bundleExclude)`. That filter (`relatedDocs.ts`) drops each link
  on its own, by a glob match on the linked document's path.
  - **Today's patterns.** Every pattern names
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/...`. At `85ba619f` that folder holds
  0 files and `ChatHistory/` holds 359, so today's patterns leave nothing out
  there.
  - **The step's 25 links, from the cached summaries** (commit `85ba619f`
  against its parent `c06c2ccb`):
    - Added: 24, every one `<file> ->
  AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/2026-10-31.1.record.md`, each matching
  the old pattern `AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/**/!(*.SUMMARIZED).md`.
    - Removed: 1, `forceGraphReveal.ts ->
  AI-Agent-Workspace/Screenshots/2026-10-28/README.md`, which no pattern, old or
  new, ever excluded.
  - **Why filtering the cache stands in for the old summary.** Today's patterns
  exclude nothing at that commit, and no cited-by entry in any of the 1,263
  cached summaries names a `ChatHistory` document (0 found). So that commit
  summarized under the old configuration equals today's summary with the old
  patterns applied as a filter.
  - **The step under the old patterns.** Added links go from 24 to 0, so the
  "Documents linked" group disappears. The one removed link stays, so "Documents
  unlinked" remains. The groups are then exactly the case's six.

  ## How many steps
  Applying the old patterns to the current `history.json` (1,223 steps):
  - 188 steps change: 1,318 links added and the same 1,318 removed, across 88
  archive documents.
  - 103 steps lose their whole "Documents linked" group, 15 lose their whole
  "Documents unlinked" group, and 1 loses both.
  - The first affected step is `d2394c74` (2025-11-17, 64 of 206 added links).
  The worst is the move itself, `796ee87c` (2026-11-18), which shows 1,065 of
  its 1,408 "unlinked" entries only because the archive moved.
  - 910 of the 1,263 cached summaries carry at least one such link (416,607
  link-moments in all).
  - Summary files named `*.SUMMARIZED.md` under `ChatHistory/` were linked under
  the old patterns too (64 of them), so they are correct as they stand.

  ## The lens key
  It includes the whole configuration. `lensOf`
  (`packages/engine/src/live-docs/history/lens.ts`) hashes the generator
  identity together with the entire normalized configuration.
  - **Editing `bundleExclude` never serves stale summaries silently.** Any edit,
  even one line, makes a new key, a new empty cache folder, and the full cold
  rebuild of about two hours.
  - **Code coverage** (`generatorIdentity.ts`, `keysTheLens`): every file of
  `packages/engine` and `packages/generator`, plus `tsconfig.base.json` and the
  `package-lock.json` entry. It leaves out tests and the history-folder modules
  that only read summaries; the exceptions are the summarizing modules
  (`summary.ts`, `textStore.ts`, `summarizeChange.ts`, `git.ts`,
  `regenerate.ts`), which are keyed.
  - **Unkeyed code that still moves the key.** `computeHistory.ts` and `lens.ts`
  are not keyed, so editing them costs no rebuild. But changing the formula in
  `lensOf` changes every key's value, which is a rebuild all the same.
  - **The configuration module is keyed.** `normalizeLiveDocumentationConfig`
  drops fields it does not know and lives in keyed code. So a new configuration
  field cannot reach the history without a rebuild.

  ## Where the exclusion is applied today
  1. When a commit is summarized: forward doc links in `summarizeChange.ts`, and
  the generator's Cited By section (`generator.ts:165`, `citingDocuments(...,
  bundleExclude)`). Both are recorded in the summary.
  2. When the bundle is built: `staticBuilder.ts:136`, for today's related
  documents only.
  3. Not when the sequence is written from the summaries (`computeHistory.ts`).
  The sequence copies the summaries' links unfiltered.

  ## Why the replay-time filter idea does not avoid a rebuild
  Filtering where the sequence is written from the summaries is possible in
  unkeyed code. But the old layout has to come from somewhere:
  - Naming it in `bundleExclude` moves the key, so a rebuild.
  - Naming it in a new field means changing the keyed configuration module, so a
  rebuild.
  - Reading it raw from the file in `computeHistory.ts` would be a side door
  honoured by the history alone, which `AGENTS.md` rules out.
  - Taking `bundleExclude` out of the key while summaries stay filtered by it
  would serve stale summaries whenever a pattern is removed. That is incorrect.

  ## Alternatives for the design question (not decided)
  **When a repository moves a folder its configuration excludes, what should an
  old moment show?**

  - **A. Leave it as it is.** Today's patterns, applied literally. Old moments
  show records as linked documents: 188 noisy steps, and the move step shows
  1,065 "unlinked". The case would be moved to the new output. This costs
  nothing, but it goes against what the configuration means, and every
  repository that moves an excluded folder gets this silently.
  - **B. The configuration names the former layout as well.** Add back
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/**/!(*.SUMMARIZED).md` and
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/**/*.txt`.
    - It keeps both "One lens" (today's tool and today's configuration read
  every commit) and "never hardcode the layout" (the knowledge lives in this
  repository's configuration).
    - It matches nothing today, so today's bundle does not change.
    - It costs one full rebuild, which can ride with the rebuild already owed.
    - The burden falls on whoever moves an excluded folder: they must keep the
  old patterns. The guide could say so.
  - **C. Filter when the sequence is written, and take `bundleExclude` out of
  the key.**
    - Summaries would keep every link, and the generator inside the summarizer
  would run with no bundle exclusion, so cited-by entries stay complete too.
  `computeHistory` would then apply today's `bundleExclude` to the links and to
  cited-by when it writes the sequence, and `lensOf` would leave `bundleExclude`
  out of the key.
    - After one rebuild, any later `bundleExclude` edit (including B's) costs a
  replay of minutes, not two hours.
    - The meaning is unchanged, but the change is wider: the key's formula, the
  summarizer, the sequence writer and their tests.
  - **D. Follow moves.** Match each past document by the path it has today,
  through git rename detection.
    - Automatic, no configuration burden.
    - But it means rename detection over documents across about 1,263 commits,
  it is undefined for documents since deleted, and the product would be guessing
  intent.
    - I would not recommend it.
  - **E. Each commit's own configuration.** The owner already rejected this on
  2026-10-16 ("One lens").

  **Recommendation:** B, landed with the owed rebuild. Weigh C for the same
  rebuild, so that this repository and any other can edit `bundleExclude` later
  without paying two hours. Until then the case stays red, and it is right as it
  stands.

  ## Unfinished / not checked
  - I did not check in the browser what the reader does when someone clicks one
  of these old record links in the step dialog. I expect the bundle does not
  carry them under their old names.
  - I did not run the summarizer itself under the old configuration. The
  equivalence above rests on reading the code and on 0 `ChatHistory` cited-by
  entries across the whole cache.
  - The scripts that produced the numbers are `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live
  -Documentation/a0685a3a-b7de-45e3-bcf9-44c55d10db4e/scratchpad/diff.js`,
  `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/a0685a3a-b7de-45e3-bcf9-44c55
  d10db4e/scratchpad/steps.mjs`, `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentatio
  n/a0685a3a-b7de-45e3-bcf9-44c55d10db4e/scratchpad/scan.js` and
  `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/a0685a3a-b7de-45e3-bcf9-44c55
  d10db4e/scratchpad/count.mjs`. They read a copy of the cache, so to re-run
  them, copy `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.mdmd/history/` into the worktree
  first.

● The history case's cause is found, and it's the lead I described. Nothing is
  wrong in the product's code; this repository's configuration is at fault.

  - What happened. The configuration tells the Explorer to leave chat records
    out, by folder. Since the archive moved on November 18 it names only the new
    folder, so at old commits the records under ChatHistory/ count as "linked
    documents" again.
  - How far it reaches. 188 of the history's 1,223 steps show such links. The
    worst is the move's own commit, where 1,065 of its 1,408 "unlinked" entries
    are there only because the folder moved.
  - The test is right and stays red. It was written when the configuration still
    named the old folder.
  - Every fix costs a full rebuild. The history's cache is keyed on the whole
    configuration, so even a one-line edit starts a cold two-hour rebuild. (My
    idea of filtering later, without a rebuild, does not work.)

  What I decided. I am not paying for a rebuild for this alone. The fix goes
  into the next engine round, which already owes one rebuild, in two parts:

  1. The configuration names the archive's old folder as well as its new one.
  2. The exclusion is applied later in the pipeline, and taken out of the cache
     key, so that the next configuration edit costs minutes instead of two
     hours.

  Neither changes what the product means, so I'm telling you rather than asking.

  In hand now. The same builder is measuring where a rebuild's two hours and
  half a terabyte go, and costing three ways of making it cheap. I'll bring that
  to you as a costed design, with what each option costs in complexity, before
  anything is built.

  The other three builders (the four map cases, and the two halves of your
  packet) are still out.

› Message from general-purpose
  ## In short
  Almost all of a rebuild's disk writes are files the generator never opens.
  Writing only what is read is the change to build first, with the rebuild
  already owed. I would not build the moving tree. I would not build per-version
  analysis until the generator's index pass has been measured in more detail.

  I built nothing in the product, started no rebuild, and only read the main
  checkout's cache. One file is committed on my branch,
  `worktree-agent-a4f78f17415dc6ce3` at `167cba33` (one commit on top of
  `892833fc`, no amend, no push): `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktr
  ees/agent-a4f78f17415dc6ce3/AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/202
  6-11-19/Probes/history-rebuild-cost/README.md`. It holds the full tables
  below.

  ## How I measured
  - I ran the main checkout's build of 15:17, the one that made cache
  `c186af7356b58191`.
  - A tree was written with the built `workingTree`. The built `summarizeChange`
  ran in its own process with the tree's git environment, and I timed its
  generator, summary and text-store steps apart by wrapping those functions.
  - Three other builders shared the 20-core machine; the load was 1.9 to 4.9.
  - Four commits summarized afresh matched the cached summaries byte for byte
  once unzipped: the early, middle and head commits below, and `85ba619f`.

  ## 1. One commit's anatomy
  Wall seconds / processor seconds (user plus system, child processes included):

  | Commit | Tree | Writing the tree | Summarizing process | Of which the
  generator | Removing the tree | Writing as a share of wall time |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | `87b9e7b5`, early | 1,213 files, 16.7 MB | 2.3 / 2.5 | 10.9 / 13.3 | 9.5 /
  11.8 | 0.05 s | 17% |
  | `535ceefc`, middle | 3,020 files, 258 MB | 5.7 / 7.1 | 16.5 / 20.4 | 13.1 /
  16.4 | 0.1 s | 26% |
  | `c84fd00f`, head | 7,532 files, 1,017 MB | 17.2–28.5 / 25–39 (three runs) |
  23.4–27.4 / 29–34 | 18.5–21.6 / 22–26 | 0.3–0.5 s | 42–51% |

  - **Summary step:** 0.01 to 0.09 s, negligible.
  - **Text store:** 0.4 to 3.7 s here, but every run wrote into an empty store.
  In a real rebuild existing texts are skipped, so this overstates it.
  - **Peak memory:** 390 to 464 MB.
  - **Inside the generator**, at the head and the middle:
    - the workspace index pass: 9.3 s and 7.6 s;
    - per-file analysis: 4.7 s and 2.3 s (1,091 and 858 files);
    - discovery: 2.0 s and 0.4 s;
    - reading and writing the graph: about 0.4 s;
    - Cited By and rendering: about 0.1 s;
    - the rest (startup, reading the old docs, pruning): about 2 s.

  ## 2. What the generator opens
  At the head, under `strace`, the summarizing process opened 2,225 of the 7,532
  files: 14.4 MB of 1,017 MB, about 1.4%.
  - **`AI-Agent-Workspace/`:** 5,287 files and 1,002 MB written, and 0 files
  opened.
  - **Pictures:** 3,509 files and 711 MB. Only 15 were opened, all small fixture
  images under `tests/integration/fixtures/slopcop-assets/`, and none under the
  agent workspace.
  - **Code:** 890 of 1,035 files opened.
  - **Live Docs:** 1,113 of 1,115 opened.
  - **Git:** the `git check-ignore` children open nothing in the tree.

  At the old commit `85ba619f` nothing under `AI-Agent-Workspace/` was read
  either. That folder is touched in only two ways:
  - Discovery walks its directories (850 directory opens).
  - The summarizer checks that each linked document exists (114 `statx` calls on
  records and summaries).

  ## 3. How much is new at each commit
  Over the first-parent line up to `c84fd00f` (1,224 commits). "Analysed" means
  the file has a doc in that commit's cached summary.

  | Count | File-moments (path, version) | Bytes | Distinct versions |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Every file: what a cold rebuild writes | 4,632,597 | 521.5 GB | 28,902 (1.64
  GB) |
  | Outside `AI-Agent-Workspace/` | 1,995,516 | 11.0 GB | 20,954 |
  | Outside it, plus every markdown file under it | 2,592,249 | 51.3 GB | 24,524
  |
  | New against the previous commit | 34,276 | 2.56 GB | — |
  | New against the previous commit, outside the folder | 21,505 | 0.35 GB | — |
  | New, outside it plus its markdown | 25,865 | 0.71 GB | — |
  | Analysed files | 977,305 | 6.9 GB | 7,203 (232 MB) |

  - 7,721 distinct (path, version) pairs among the analysed files.
  - Analysed files new per commit: median 0, 90th percentile 18, at most 209.
  - So 99.2% of per-file analyses repeat a version already analysed.

  ## 4. What depends on a file's bytes alone
  - **Bytes alone:** the TypeScript parse, the exported symbols with their doc
  comments, the raw import specifiers, the binary sniff, and the
  path-and-configuration part of the archetype.
  - **The rest of that commit's tree:**
    - the index pass (the symbol index, and the entry index from every
  manifest);
    - resolving dependencies against the files on disk;
    - re-exported symbols, which read the providers;
    - DOM and route inference;
    - every language adapter, which is given the file and symbol indexes, and
  several of which mix reading facts with resolving them;
    - Cited By, the authored block from the commit's own docs, whether linked
  documents exist, and the graph read back from all the docs.
  - **What of October 19 exists in the code:** the extraction-versus-resolution
  boundary is a principle, not a seam. Only caches that live for one run exist.
  There is no saved form of unresolved facts and no store across runs. The
  October 19 prototype saved nothing and was removed. The history's per-commit
  process can reuse nothing across commits today.

  ## The three shapes
  **(i) Write only what is read.**
  - **Saves.** Writes fall from 521 GB to 11 GB (98%), and most of the 17 to 28
  s of tree writing at late commits goes. Early commits gain little. At the
  head's rate the line's writes are about 3.6 to 5.6 of the rebuild's 22
  processor-hours.
  - **Needs.** The tree writer leaves out what `exclude` names, using
  discovery's own matcher. The check that a linked document exists must ask the
  commit's file listing, not the disk. Otherwise the markdown under the excluded
  folder must still be written: 51 GB, because records are large.
  - **Cost.** Small. It touches `git.ts` and `summarizeChange.ts`, which are
  both in the lens key, so it rides with the owed rebuild.
  - **Risk.** A future reader of excluded files would silently see less. The
  oracle comparison guards against that.

  **(ii) Move one tree from commit to commit.**
  - **Saves.** Writes fall to 2.6 GB alone. After (i), only 0.35 to 0.7 GB of 11
  GB remains to save, about 2% of the original writes.
  - **Needs.** Each process must take a contiguous run of the line in order.
  Today the pool hands commits to whichever process frees first, so neighbours
  land in different processes. Contiguous runs lose load balancing, since late
  commits cost three to five times early ones.
  - **Risk.**
    - The generator's own writes must be cleaned between commits.
    - Either git checkout's rules for names, links and case replace the careful
  tree writer's, or the difference-applying code must be written anew.
    - A leftover from the previous commit could silently enter a summary.
  - **Verdict.** Do not build.

  **(iii) Analyse each distinct version once.**
  - **Ceiling.** Large: 977,305 file-moments rest on 7,203 versions.
  - **The time it can reach.** The index pass plus per-file analysis is 75% of
  the generator. How much of that is reading facts, as against resolving them,
  starting up, loading grammars, or the 2.5 s pause October 19 found after the
  PowerShell adapter, is unmeasured.
  - **Cost.** Large: a saved form of every adapter's unresolved facts, a store
  keyed by version and code identity, and a second pass that resolves only from
  those facts. The far end, reading from git's object store with no tree, also
  replaces the disk access every adapter uses.
  - **Risk.** A cached fact that secretly depended on its neighbours, which
  October 19 found twice.
  - **Verdict.** Do not build it until the index pass is broken down. If it
  turns out to be a fixed cost per process, there is a cheaper fix: the pause
  alone is about 50 processor-minutes across the line.

  **How they compose.** (i) composes with both (ii) and (iii); (iii) with no
  tree on disk would make (i) and (ii) unnecessary.

  ## Recommendation
  1. Build (i), with the owed rebuild.
  2. Then measure the index pass: parse, resolution and fixed cost per process.
  3. Do not build (ii).
  4. Leave (iii) until step 2 says it pays.

  ## The oracle
  - **Storage.** Summaries are gzipped JSON with `format`, `lens`, `commit`,
  `files` and `docLinks`. Texts are content-addressed, so identical summaries
  name identical texts.
  - **Deterministic in practice.** Four of four matched. Compare unzipped bytes,
  not gzip bytes.
  - **A changed lens key.** Any of the shapes moves the key, so new summaries
  carry another `lens` value: set that one field aside when comparing. The
  comparison must also run under the oracle's own configuration, because the
  configuration is in the key. Configuration changes such as your options B and
  C are a separate step, compared on their own.
  - **As a test.** A full comparison over 1,224 commits is itself a rebuild. A
  sample of about 50 commits takes roughly 15 to 20 minutes at four processes,
  using a driver like this probe's that hands `summarizeChange` the oracle's
  lens and configuration. It must never write into the main checkout's cache.

  ## Not measured
  - How the pool behaves at twelve processes, and so the wall-clock saving of
  (i) under it.
  - The inside of the index pass, and the PowerShell pause.
  - The text store in a warm rebuild.
  - Bytes actually written to the disk device (mine are logical sizes from git
  and `du`).
  - More than three commits for the anatomy, and more than one commit for each
  of the two traces.

  The scripts are in `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/a0685a3a-b
  7de-45e3-bcf9-44c55d10db4e/scratchpad/probe/`; I deleted their run outputs and
  the trees.

  no commits.

  ## Stage 1, commit `df0f792b`
  **1. Write only what is read.**
  - `regenerate.ts` gains `unread(configuration)`. It leaves out what `exclude`
  names, using discovery's own matcher (`matchesPathPattern`). It never leaves
  out anything under the docs root, because the generator reads and rewrites
  that whatever `exclude` says.
  - The tree writer (`writeStoredTree` / `workingTree` in `git.ts`) takes that
  rule as `leaveOut`. Left-out files are not written and not named as unwritten.
  The rule is checked last, so an entry refused for its name, its kind or a
  duplicate is still named even under an excluded folder.
  - The head check's tree uses the same rule.
  - The summarizer (`summarizeChange.ts`) decides whether a linked document
  exists by asking the commit's own listing (`git ls-files --stage` against the
  tree's index): regular files, minus the unwritten entries. It no longer asks
  the disk.

  **2. Your C.**
  - The summarizer gives its generator and its linked-document reading
  `bundleExclude: []`, so a summary keeps every linked and citing document.
  - One new function, `bundledSummary` in
  `packages/engine/src/live-docs/history/bundled.ts`, takes the excluded
  documents out of the links and out of each file's Cited By. When a Cited By
  list empties, its key is dropped, as the summarizer already does for an empty
  list. It uses the bundle's own matcher: `excluded` in `relatedDocs.ts`, now
  exported.
  - `computeHistory`'s `buildSequence` applies it to every summary as it reads
  it, and the head check compares that filtered sequence.
  - The headless twin (`inspect --at`, `--between`) reads the sequence file, not
  summaries, so it gets the filtered data with no change.
  - `lensOf` now hashes the configuration minus `bundleExclude`; the lens still
  carries the whole configuration for its reader.
  - **What the key covers now:** the generator's version, its committed keyed
  code (unchanged `keysTheLens`), the tool lock, uncommitted changes to those,
  the Unicode version, and every configuration field except `bundleExclude`. The
  code changes in this commit move the key, which is expected and rides with
  the owed rebuild.

  **3. The three owed mends.**
  - **No links.** The tree writer writes no symbolic link at all. Each link is
  named unwritten: "it is a symbolic link, and the history writes none, so that
  nothing it writes or reads goes through one". This closes three findings of
  the fourth review: a link at the docs root, an empty or NUL target failing the
  commit, and Windows `EPERM`. The link-writing code is deleted. No commit on
  this repository's first-parent line holds a link, so no fact here changes.
  - **Folder memory.** `placeEntry` remembers the folders this writer has made
  or checked.
  - **`execArgv`.** The fork passes `execArgv: summarizingFlags(worker)`:
  nothing for the built JavaScript, and only tsx's loader (`--import <tsx>`) for
  TypeScript. The stale words about the environment in `regenerate.ts` are
  corrected.

  **Tests**, each written to fail without its change; I did not run them against
  the old code to watch them fail:
  - `bundled.test.ts`, new.
  - `lens.test.ts`: editing `bundleExclude` keeps the key, editing `exclude`
  moves it.
  - `git.test.ts`: no link written, including empty, NUL and docs-root links; a
  caller's leave-out rule honoured while refusals there are still named; each
  folder asked once. The link expectations in the case-folding and checkout
  cases are updated.
  - `generatorIdentity.test.ts`: the guard now lists `summarizeChange.ts` among
  the modules that start a process; a fork-flags case and an `unread` case are
  added.
  - The integration "thousands of entries left out" case now uses 3,000 links
  instead of one link over 3,000 files.

  ## What a reviewer should attack first
  1. **The listing in place of the disk** (`standingFiles` in
  `summarizeChange.ts`). It must say "stands" exactly where the disk used to:
  regular files only, minus unwritten entries, with the paths from `git
  ls-files` relative to the tree under the tree's environment variables.
  2. **`unread` and discovery.** `unread` copies discovery's `exclude` matcher,
  but discovery also walks folders and runs a nested-scan glob (`scansWithin`)
  that ignores `exclude`. A `.live-docs.config.json` under an excluded folder
  now goes unseen, which only changes a warning. Look for any other reader of
  excluded files.
  3. **The docs-root carve-out in `unread`.** It is the one place the rule is
  not just `exclude`.
  4. **`bundledSummary` reproducing the old key order and absent keys** when a
  Cited By list empties. This repository's sample never had an excluded citing
  document, so that branch is covered only by the unit test.
  5. **The tsx flags.** `require.resolve("tsx")` gives `dist/loader.mjs`, and
  `TSX_TSCONFIG_PATH` is still passed in the environment. Are the old inherited
  flags needed anywhere else?
  6. **`placeEntry`'s folder memory on case-insensitive disks.** A remembered
  folder is skipped by its exact spelling only; another spelling is still asked
  of the disk.

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Index pass measurement: most of its time is one wait, and it is not parsing

  Most of the index pass is a single wait. After the first few PowerShell
  parses, V8 decides that a function in the PowerShell grammar's WebAssembly is
  hot. It recompiles it with its optimizing compiler, TurboFan. That compile
  burns **5–11 s of one background thread**, and the main thread sits blocked
  with no processor time until it finishes.

  Running the same generation with `node --liftoff-only` changes three things:
  - The pause disappears.
  - The index pass falls from 10.0–17.7 s to 4.3–4.7 s of wall time, and from
  14–25 to about 8 processor-seconds.
  - Peak memory falls by 150–200 MB.

  Everything else is small next to that.

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a2b35813d4764cac9`, tip `5652967c`, one new commit
  on `b55cc081`. It adds only `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2
  026-11-19/Probes/generation-index-pass/README.md`. Nothing under `packages/`
  or in the configuration changed; nothing was pushed or amended. I used no
  subagent, so there was no independent review.

  **How it was measured.**
  - **Trees.** Three commit trees were written with the history's own tree
  writer (`workingTree` of the built `git.js`): `87b9e7b5` (534 files),
  `535ceefc` (858) and `b55cc081` (1,091).
  - **Runs.** Each was generated with the built `generateLiveDocs` in a process
  of its own, in the tree's git environment, under the head's configuration. The
  final set are dry runs, because the old commits' committed docs fail today's
  graph reader.
  - **Driver.** It wrapped the writable CommonJS exports (the phases,
  `analyzeWithLanguageAdapters`, each adapter's `analyze`) and
  `fs/promises.readFile`. It recorded processor time per process and per thread
  from `/proc/self/task`.
  - **Profile.** `node --cpu-prof` at 500 µs, aligned to the driver's clock with
  a named busy-loop marker. Samples were classed by stack, and the index pass
  was split into per-file windows, since it runs one file at a time.
  - **Load.** The load average was 10–16 throughout from other builders, so wall
  times are indicative. I report processor seconds and the with/without
  comparison instead.

  The scripts are in `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/a0685a3a-b
  7de-45e3-bcf9-44c55d10db4e/scratchpad/bin/` (`driver.cjs`, `analyze.cjs`,
  `run.sh`, `pause.cjs`, `grammar-pause.cjs`, `mktrees.cjs`). The scratch trees
  and run outputs have been removed.

  ### 1. The index pass decomposed

  These are main-thread seconds at the head, default flags / `--liftoff-only`.
  The early and middle commits are in the README.

  | Kind of work | Head, seconds |
  | --- | ---: |
  | Waiting with nothing to run | 6.07 / 0.41 |
  | TypeScript parse | 2.34 / 2.65 |
  | tree-sitter parse, all grammars | 0.18 / 0.21 |
  | Adapter extraction (JSON is the largest) | about 0.5 |
  | TypeScript symbol extraction | about 0.15 |
  | Reading files | 0.21 / 0.23 |
  | Loading grammars and the runtime | under 0.05 |

  At the early and middle commits the wait was 13.4 and 12.4 s.

  - **Processor time.** The index pass took 14.3 processor-seconds; V8's worker
  threads were 8.9 s of that. At the early and middle commits the totals were
  24.6 and 25.3, of which the workers were 16.8 and 16.6. Under Liftoff-only the
  pass takes about 8 processor-seconds, of which the workers are about 3.
  - **Resolution** inside the index pass is under 0.1 s. In the analysis pass it
  is 0.73 s.
  - **Loading a grammar alone is cheap:** PowerShell 64 ms, C# 71 ms, C++ 106
  ms, Python 39 ms.

  ### 2. The PowerShell pause

  **It is still there, and larger than October's 2.5 s:** 5.0–6.1 s at the head
  over four runs, and 10.8–11.1 s at the early and middle commits.

  **What it is.** It is a TurboFan compile of a WebAssembly function in the
  PowerShell grammar.
  - **In isolation**, two parses of the 23 KB `powershell-ast.ps1` take about
  120 ms. The next event-loop turn then takes 11–13 s, all of it one V8 worker
  thread's processor time, with 0 ms on the main thread.
  - **Under `--liftoff-only`** that turn takes 2 ms.
  - **Under `--no-liftoff`** the same 10.8 s lands on the main thread inside the
  parse instead.
  - **It is not our patch:** the engine's patched grammar and the bundled
  `@vscode/tree-sitter-wasm` grammar behave the same.
  - **It is not** a garbage collection, a file read, the grammar's load, or
  anything in the adapter's JavaScript.
  - **The C++ grammar** shows a smaller pause of the same kind, 0.5–1.2 s.
  - **Liftoff alone parses PowerShell about 2× slower** (20 parses: 609 ms
  against 326 ms). At this repository's 17 PowerShell files that is a few tens
  of milliseconds.

  **Across a history rebuild** of 1,224 per-commit processes, the pause costs
  about **1.7–3.7 processor-hours of the 22**.

  ### 3. Work done twice

  - **Every TypeScript and JavaScript file is parsed twice:** once in the index
  pass (2.0–2.7 s) and again in `analyzeSourceFile` (1.0–1.9 s). Providers are
  parsed a third time inside `augmentWithReExportedSymbols`. Parsing each file
  once would save about 1.0–1.9 s of main-thread time, 5–10% of a Liftoff-only
  generation. It needs either keeping every AST alive between the passes (memory
  not measured) or the extraction seam that does not exist yet.
  - **Adapters run in both passes,** but most keep their own facts within the
  process. At the head their second pass cost 0.15 s in all.
  - **Reads.** At the head, 944 of the 2,213 paths read were read more than once
  (3,561 calls in all): every TypeScript file twice, C# files and C headers
  three times, some JSON four times. In a writing run every existing Live Doc is
  also read twice. All reading together is 0.2 s of the index pass and 0.5–0.9
  s of the analysis pass, so a read cache would save well under a second.

  ### 4. How it grows with files

  Under Liftoff-only, the index pass took 4.25, 4.34 and 4.72 s at 534, 858 and
  1,091 files. Its TypeScript parse took 2.04, 2.29 and 2.65 s, about 30% growth
  for twice the files.

  - **Fixed per process:** the TurboFan pauses, 5–11 s, paid whenever a
  PowerShell (or C++) file is parsed at all, whatever the file count. Also
  grammar loading (under 0.4 s for all grammars) and a memory baseline of about
  200 MB.
  - **Grows with files:** both TypeScript parses, and the analysis pass's
  resolution and composition. The analysis pass ran 4.7–7.1 s, too noisy under
  this load to give a slope.

  ### 5. Memory

  | Peak resident memory, dry run | Early | Middle | Head |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | Default flags | 424 MB | 385 MB | 403 MB |
  | Liftoff-only | 224 MB | 232 MB | 253 MB |

  - **Writing runs** at the head, with the profiler running, peaked at 497–510
  MB.
  - **Most of the default figure is the TurboFan compile.**
  - **Per file:** roughly 50 KB above a baseline of about 200 MB. That is a
  slope from three points under load, so treat it as rough. At that rate even
  20,000 files would stay near 1.2 GB, comfortable on an 8 GB machine.

  ### 6. What follows

  1. **A plain waste, removable with identical results: turn off the WebAssembly
  optimizing tier.**
     - **How:** `--liftoff-only` or `--no-wasm-tier-up` on the processes the
  tool forks, `NODE_OPTIONS` for the CLI, or `v8.setFlagsFromString` before the
  first grammar loads.
     - **Why results stay identical:** it changes only how V8 compiles the
  grammar's machine code, never what the parser produces.
     - **What it saves:** 5–11 s of wall time, 7–17 processor-seconds and
  150–200 MB of memory per process; about 2–4 processor-hours of a rebuild.
     - **What it costs:** one line and a comment saying why.
     - **Before landing, check** that `--no-wasm-tier-up` gives the same relief,
  since only `--liftoff-only` was measured. Also check that a repository heavy
  in PowerShell or C++ parses fast enough on Liftoff alone.
     - **Do this first.**
  2. **Parsing each TypeScript file once** would save about 1–2 s of the roughly
  14 s left per process. It costs either AST memory or the extraction seam. Not
  worth doing on its own.
  3. **A store of per-version results** could reach at most about 4–5 s of a
  10–14 s process: the parses and extraction. It cannot reach discovery,
  resolution, composition, Cited By or process start. It needs the full seam
  between extracting a file's facts and resolving them, and it carries October's
  risk of a cached fact that secretly depends on its neighbours. **Don't build
  it now.** Remove the pause, write only what is read (shape (i) of the
  history-rebuild probe), then measure a rebuild again.
  4. **Don't build** a read cache, or an AST cache between the passes before its
  memory is measured.

  ### What was not measured

  - **Machine and repetitions.** Nothing was measured on an idle machine, and
  each configuration in the final set ran once.
  - **WebAssembly flags.** Neither `--no-wasm-tier-up` nor
  `--wasm-dynamic-tiering` was tried.
  - **The pause's internals.** I did not find which WebAssembly function is
  compiled (likely the generated lexer), or why the main thread blocks on that
  compile.
  - **AST-cache memory** was not measured.
  - **Writing runs at old commits** were not possible: today's graph reader
  rejects their committed docs.
  - **The history's own `summarizeChange` process** was not run. Process start
  and the text store are outside these figures.
  - **No browser** was needed.

    your work's code is PowerShell. A builder is finding why the wait happens

  ### 3 and 4. `local-map-search.spec.ts:65` and `local-map-hidden.spec.ts:51`:
  the repository grew, and the 2D search is slow at the new size
  The 2D map's code (`views/localView/`) is identical in both commits.

  | Search on the five files | Old docs | New docs |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 80 seeds (the hidden case's tuning) | 22.5 s: 80 seeds + 90 boundary joins,
  9 adoptions | 73.7 s: 80 seeds + 231 joins, 29 adoptions |
  | Cost per trial | 132 ms | 237 ms |
  | Picture | 93 cards, 546 rows | 98 cards, 627 rows |
  | 4 seeds (the search case's first competition; the case then needs a second
  one after a pin change) | 12.8 s, 90 joins | 37.3 s, 185 joins |

  - **Why it is longer:** every adoption makes a fresh list of joins, so the
  search tries nearly twice as many pictures. Each one also costs nearly twice
  as much, because the placement is bigger.
  - **Where the time goes** (CPU profile mapped through the source map): the
  placement's network simplex is 73% of the search, inside `layoutScene` at 87%.
  Within the solver:
    - rebuilding the tree's postorder every pivot: 16.2 s
    - growing the tight tree: 12.8 s
    - finding the entering edge: 12.4 s
    - cut values: 8.6 s
    - measuring the page: 14.7 s
  - **Built: one plain saving** (`f6cf242b`). Each pivot now works out the
  leaving edge's lower end once (`headSide`), where each side-of-cut test had
  found it again twice.
    - On 252 random systems, every result was identical as JSON and the solver
  took 18% less time.
    - The 464 2D unit cases pass.
    - On the page, the 80-seed search ended exactly as before: 80 seeds, 231
  joins, 29 adoptions, seed 77, final price 3,197,457.
    - The page gain is small: about 6% of the search, estimated by allowing for
  the machine's load (7.5 against 3.9).
    - Both cases were rerun on the mended client and are still red.
  - **Fork for you, not built:**
    - (a) **Recommended first:** an incremental network simplex. It would update
  the postorder and cut values only for the part of the tree the exchange
  moves, as Gansner and Graphviz do. The pivots stay the same, so the pictures
  stay identical. It targets about a third of the solver time. The cost is about
  100 lines of delicate bookkeeping in the core solver, checked against today's
  solver with an equivalence harness.
    - (b) Change when the search stops, for example not renewing the join list
  after a join is adopted, or a trial budget. This changes which pictures are
  found, which is the owner's stopping rule.
    - (c) Keep both cases red by the owner's rule until (a) is measured.

  ## Verdict: I stopped after the measurement and built nothing in the engine

  The recorded rule was "every word that names a code file is an entry, reading
  into quoted command lines". On 983 real scripts it found **0** starts that
  today's rule misses. It added **13 false** entries and **2 arguable** ones.
  Its notion of "code" lost **2 true** starts that today finds.

  Every false entry is a tool's argument:
  - an output: `terser index.js -c -m -o min.js` gives `min.js`; also `uglifyjs
  … --output …` and `browserify … -o umd/…`
  - a `cp` target (4 of them)
  - a file a script rewrites (`node ../../tools/dedupe-types.js
  dist/cjs/index.cjs dist/esm/index.js`)

  Only a list of each tool's flags could tell those apart from a file that is
  run.

  The verdict holds whatever "code" means. With today's filter (anything that
  isn't a tooling input) in place of a code filter, the rule adds 15 false
  entries and still finds nothing new.

  It also hits this repository. If the tokenizer splits `(` and `)` as a shell
  does, the engine manifest gets a false entry in its history. From late
  September to October 16, 2026, the engine's `build` copied
  `powershell.emit-ast.ps1` inside `node -e "…copyFileSync('src/…', 'dist/…')"`,
  and the history summaries (which carry entries) would show that build
  starting the PowerShell emitter. Keeping parentheses inside words avoids this
  only by luck.

  Both of your stop conditions are met.

  ## What was measured
  - **This repository:** 96 distinct script texts over main's first-parent line
  (32 at head, 64 earlier), from 181 manifest versions under 12 manifest paths.
  Each was resolved against the tree of the newest commit that held it, with

  ## Totals

  | Rule | Real scripts (983): adds T / F / A | Real: removes F / A, loses T |
  Constructed (43): adds T / F / A | Constructed: removes F, loses T |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Recorded | 0 / 13 / 2 | 5 / 1, 2 | 11 / 2 / 1 | 7, 2 |

  | **B** | 0 / 0 / 0 | 5 / 1, 0 | 9 / 1 / 0 | 3, 0 |

  All the gains on real scripts come from the tokenizer, not from any rule: a
  file after `<`, `>`, `>>`, `&>` or `<<<` is not a word of the command. That
  removes 5 false entries today makes (bundles written by redirection) and 1
  arguable one.

  ## Forks decided
  **What "code" means.** The engine has only three facts to draw the line with:
  `isToolingFile`, `hasReader`, and the JSON adapter's own files. "A reader
  reads it, not tooling, not JSON" drops `data/input.json`, `config/dev.env` and
  `check.log`. But it also drops shell scripts and extensionless executables,
  which today's rule finds. On real scripts that means http-server's `node
  ./bin/http-server` and ajv's `scripts/prepare-tests`. The engine cannot tell
  `bin/www` from `.env` without a list of extensions or reading the file.

  **The tokenizer.** It keeps a backslash before an ordinary character, so `node
  scripts\a.ts` stays right. It already is right today, because the adapter
  turns `\` into `/`.

  ## The alternatives (no list of launchers in any)
  1. **B (my recommendation).** Today's rule read through a tokenizer that
  understands quotes, comments, redirections and `&`. A quoted word that holds
  whitespace and is not itself a file is read as a command of its own. A bare
  `--` begins a new command when the next word is a program name (not a flag,
  not an assignment, not path-like).
     - Gains: every start the reviews or the wrappers' documentation name,
  including dotenv `-- vite-node a.ts` and the browser lane's `-- node
  e2e-passes.mjs`. It drops the comment, here-string, redirect and
  half-quoted-path false entries.
     - Losses: none on real scripts.
     - Cost: `node gen.mjs -- go x.js` gives `x.js`. Of 38 real scripts with a
  bare `--`, 11 are followed by a program name. In 7 of those it is really an
  argument (eslint's `node Makefile.js lint -- fix`), but none is followed by a
  file.
  2. **A.** B without the `--` reading. The same as B on every real row. It
  misses the dotenv and browser-lane starts.
  3. **Recorded, not after `--` (your suggested variant).** It keeps all 13
  false entries and also loses `npm exec -- tsx a.ts`. I do not recommend it.

  **A separate small fork.** Naming `.env`, `.env.*` and `*.env` in
  `isToolingFile` would remove the dotenv/`--env-file` false entries without
  dropping shell scripts. It would also make such a file a `tooling` entry on
  its own doc. Not built.

  **Still not read under B:** `(cd x && node ../a.js)`, `%VAR%` and `$(…)`.
  Today finds `node $(npm root)/../scripts/a.js` only by an accident of the
  path. B also inherits today's own false entries that no rule fixes: `babel -o
  legacy.js index.js` gives `legacy.js`, `cp src/a.js dist/` gives `src/a.js`,
  and `echo "node b.js …"` gives `b.js`.

  ## Changed entries in this repository's docs
  None. At head, no rule changes any entry. In its history, only the engine
  `node -e` build differs, and only under the shell-paren tokenizer.

  ## Tests red before and green after
  None written, since nothing was built. The README's constructed-row table
  shows today's result for every review case, so the red/green set for B or A
  can be read straight off it.

  ## 1. Each new test, watched failing

  ## 2. One rule for "this path marks a scan"
  - `generator.ts` now exports `scanMarkers(config)`, which names a scan's two
  marks: the docs root and `.live-docs.config.json`.
  - It also exports `marksScan(relative, config)`, true when a path is that
  configuration file or lies under a docs root, at any depth.
  - `scansWithin` builds its glob from `scanMarkers`; so does `isScan`.
  - `unread` never leaves out a file that `marksScan` names. So `scansWithin`
  and `unread` share one definition of the marks, but not one predicate:
  `scansWithin` matches them by glob, `marksScan` by path segments. If you want
  literally one predicate, `scansWithin` would have to glob everything and
  filter, which is slower.
  - I touched nothing else in `generator.ts`.
  - Test: `packages/generator/src/history/regenerate.test.ts`. It writes a
  commit through the tree writer with `unread`; the commit holds
  `archive/sub/.live-docs.config.json` and
  `archive/other/.mdmd/layer-4/x.ts.mdmd.md` under an excluded `archive/`. It
  requires the generator's nested-scan warnings to equal those it gives over the
  whole commit.

  ## 3. Who asks about the excluded folder by name
  - **How it was run:** the code before `df0f792b` (the main checkout's build of
  15:17, which still asks the disk), at the head `c84fd00f` and at the early
  `87b9e7b5`.
  - **Head:** 1,489 folders opened, 4,754 `getdents64`, 8,210 `statx`, 0 files
  opened for their content.
  - **Early:** 42 folders opened, 1,267 `getdents64`, 201 `statx`, 0 files
  opened.
  - Callers were named by a preloaded hook on node's `fs` and on `glob`.

  | Caller | What it asks | Can the answer reach a summary, a warning or an exit
  code? |
  |---|---|---|
  | Discovery's walk (`glob(["**/*"])`), twice: files to document, then
  documents | every name | No. Everything found is held to `exclude` afterwards
  (`keptByGitAndConfiguration`). |
  | The nested-scan search (`scansWithin`) | docs roots and configuration files
  anywhere | Yes, a warning. Mended in item 2. |
  | The JSON adapter's `compilerPaths`, through TypeScript's
  `parseJsonConfigFileContent` (1,246 calls at the head) | TypeScript lists
  every folder under the tsconfig's folder | No. Only `outDir` and `rootDir` are
  read; the file list is discarded. Left to whoever owns `json.ts`. |
  | `stands` (`fs.stat` on linked records: 116 at the head, 55 early) | whether
  a linked document exists | Yes, a summary. Already mended in `df0f792b` by
  asking the commit's listing. |
  | `git check-ignore` | the walked paths | No. It opens nothing in the tree,
  answers from the index, and the `exclude` filter drops its answers for
  excluded paths anyway. |

  The Go, Java, Rust and C# adapters glob the whole workspace only when they are
  given no file index; the generator always gives one, and none of those globs
  ran. All of this is in the probe README as a dated section.

  ## 4. Where the listing and the disk can answer differently
  This is now written out in `standingFiles` (`summarizeChange.ts`):
  - **A file left out by `exclude`:** the disk says no, the listing yes. The
  listing is right; the file stood at the commit.
  - **A link to a file, or a document reached through a linked folder:** the
  disk followed the link and said yes; the listing says no. The listing is right
  by the no-link rule: the target is not a file that stood under that name.
  - **A disk that folds case or Unicode form:** the disk could say yes to
  another spelling; the listing answers by the exact path git holds. The listing
  is right, because a summary must not depend on a disk the lens does not key.
  - **A file the generator writes or prunes during the run:** these are only
  Live Docs and `index.json`, which are never related documents. Both answer
  alike.
  - **A folder or submodule at a document's name:** neither is a file. Both
  answer alike.
  - **An unwritten entry:** absent from the disk, and taken out of the listing.
  Both answer alike.

  No commit of this repository changes a fact through any of these:
  - The first-parent line holds no entry of mode 120000 (links) or 160000
  (submodules), counted with `git log --raw`.
  - The oracle's trees were written on a disk that tells case apart.
  - The 54-commit sample was identical.

  | Code | Runs | Result |
  |---|---|---|

  7 green |

  My unproven lead is that this is a race in the case itself, not a leak. The
  case waits for the exit of the tsx wrapper it kills, not of the history
  process under it. One such folder (`0asuPQ`, during the gate) vanished by
  itself seconds later, but the others were still there when I removed them. I
  cannot tell a race in the case from a leak in `Scratch` when a signal lands
  mid-commit. It wants the reviewer's eye, or a builder told to run it down. I
  did not change the case.

  ## Decided by me
  - The nested-scan mark is a shared definition of the marks, as described under
  item 2, not a single predicate.
  - `unread` keeps the whole docs root as well as every scan mark.
  - The `json.ts` walk is recorded but not touched, since it cannot reach
  output.
  - I did not add the WebAssembly-tier flag.

› Message from general-purpose
  **Don't rebuild on this yet.** The tree writer now holds back two kinds of
  file that the generator does read. On a repository that has either, the
  history contradicts the generator's own docs and the check at the head refuses
  to finish. This repository has neither, so a rebuild today would give the
  right data here. But every fix is in a file that keys the cache, so a rebuild
  made now would be thrown away.

  The written review is committed on my branch as `9abb0f66`: `AI-Agent-Workspac
  e/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/engine-round-review/README.md`
  (in worktree
  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-a069f07f046ffe1b6`). I
  changed no product code and left the worktree clean. I never wrote under the
  main checkout's `.mdmd/`.

  ## Findings, most serious first

  **1. Excluded files that included code imports are no longer written, so
  dependency facts change.**
  - **What happens, confirmed by running the old code (`167cba33`) and the new
  on the same test repository:**
    - The repository excludes `generated/**`, and `src/index.ts` re-exports
  `../generated/api` and imports `../generated/types`.
    - In a checkout, and from the old history command, the generator writes the
  lines `api` (re-export) and `types.thing`, plus the anchors `Api` and `api`.
    - The new command writes the raw `../generated/api` and
  `../generated/types`, and no anchors.
    - With `--check-head`, the old command exits 0 and the new exits 1
  ("dependencies … differ; reExports … differ").
  - **Why:** the generator does read excluded files.
    - `dependencies.ts:391-404` checks the disk when resolving an import;
  `:538-544` reads the target of `export *`.
    - Most language adapters check the disk the same way (C, Python, Ruby, CSS,
  HTML, C#, project files, Go, Rust). I read these but did not run each.
  - **Where:** `regenerate.ts:193-196` (`unread`). It turns "never opened",
  measured once on this repository, into a rule for every repository.
  - **Fix:** leave out only the excluded files no reader handles (`hasReader`
  false). At this repository's head that is still about 97% of the excluded
  folder's ~1.0 GB (png 711 MB, webm 156 MB, zip 58 MB, md 47 MB). About 14 MB
  of `.ts`/`.json`-type files would be written. Add an integration case with an
  import into an excluded folder.

  **2. With no symbolic link written and nothing in its place, linked files and
  documents drop out.**
  - **What happens, confirmed by run:** the test repository has three links and
  a committed mirror.

    | Link | Before (old code and generator agree) | Now |
    |---|---|---|
    | `lib/b.ts -> ../src/b.ts` | has a doc | gone |
    | `CLAUDE.md -> AGENTS.md` | cites `src/a.ts` | citation gone |
    | `docs/alias.md -> guide.md` | linked from `src/a.ts`'s authored words |
  link gone |

    With `--check-head` the new code exits 1. Every run also says these files
  "were not read, since the disk … could not hold them", which isn't true.
  - **Against the record:** the decisions log
  (`architectural-decisions.mdmd.md:614`) asked the history "to write no link at
  all, name each as not followed, **and read the file a link points to at its
  own path**". The third part wasn't built.
  - **Where:** `git.ts:301-302` and `summarizeChange.ts:141-154`.
  - **Fix:**
    - Write a link whose target is a file inside the tree as a plain copy of
  that file.
    - Leave out and name every other link: one pointing outside, at a folder,
  nowhere, or at or along the docs root.
    - Count the copies as existing files in `standingFiles`.
    - Test it with `CLAUDE.md -> AGENTS.md` and the head check.

  **3. The summary now generates with no bundle exclusions, a run no person ever
  makes. A document whose name holds a parenthesis now stops the history.**
  - **What happens, confirmed by run:** with `bundleExclude: ["notes/**"]` and a
  document `notes/plan (draft).md` that cites `src/a.ts`, the old command exits
  0 and the new exits 1.
  - **Why:** the reader rejects the `Cited By` line it now writes ("unexpected
  Cited By line", `document.ts:672`). That reader fault is older than this
  change. The change exposes it for documents the configuration had kept out.
  - **Also confirmed by run:** `--keep-index` indexes now carry `Cited By`
  entries the bundle excludes. `history.json` itself is unchanged.
  - **By reading:** `bundled.ts` filters the doc's label, which is the path with
  backticks turned into apostrophes, not the path itself.
  - **Where:** `summarizeChange.ts:84`, `compose.ts:757-761`.
  - **Fix:** write `Cited By` links so any path reads back (angle brackets or
  percent-encoding), and filter on the path.

  **4. The three behaviours this change rests on can be removed without any test
  failing.**
  - **What I ran:** each change below alone, then all 133 history unit tests and
  the 30 history integration tests. Everything passed each time; I reverted
  each.
    - (a) Asking the disk whether a linked document exists
  (`summarizeChange.ts:151`). In this repository that would drop every archive
  document link from the summaries, over a thousand per recent commit.
    - (b) Applying `bundleExclude` inside the summary again (`:84`). The cache
  would go stale after an edit, since the key no longer covers it.
    - (c) Not applying it when summaries are read (`computeHistory.ts:638`).
  - **Why nothing catches them:** the test repositories have no `exclude`, no
  `bundleExclude` and no link.
  - **Tests that do work:** the new lens test and the folder-memory test each
  failed when I took their change away. The node-flags test is a regular
  expression over the source; I didn't try removing it.

  **5. Nothing can raise the summarizing process's memory limit any more (by
  reading).**
  - **Where:** `regenerate.ts:223-225` and `:241`. `NODE_OPTIONS` was dropped
  last round and the node flags are now fixed.
  - **Fix:** pass `--max-old-space-size` through; it decides whether a summary
  is made, never what it says.

  **6. A generator warning vanishes (confirmed by run).** A nested
  `.live-docs.config.json` under an excluded folder no longer produces "A scan
  lies inside this folder…" (`generator.ts:329`, `scansWithin`).

  **7. On macOS and Windows a linked document spelled in another case or Unicode
  form no longer counts (by reading).** This makes every platform agree, which
  I think is right, but it is a change. Windows gains: links used to fail with
  `EPERM` there and stop the whole history.

● The independent review of the engine round is in, and its verdict is not to
  rebuild yet. It found two real faults, both in rules I set, and it has
  corrected something I told you at 17:45. Its file is here (AI-Agent-Workspace/
  AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/engine-round-review/README.md).

  What I got wrong

  I told you the generator opens no file in the folder the configuration
  excludes, and built "write only what is read" on that. That was a measurement
  of this repository, not a property of the tool.

  - The generator does read excluded files. When code imports from an excluded
    folder, the generator checks the disk to resolve the import. The reviewer
    built a small repository that does this: the new code lost the import's
    facts and failed its own head check, where the old code passed.
  - Writing no symbolic link at all drops facts too. A linked source file lost
    its doc, and a linked CLAUDE.md -> AGENTS.md lost its citations.

  On this repository neither occurs, so a rebuild today would have given right
  data here: 85 sampled commits match the old cache exactly. But both fixes are
  in code the cache is keyed on, so a rebuild now would have been thrown away.
  That is what reviewing before the rebuild was for.

  What I ruled

  - Excluded files: every file's existence stays exact. An excluded file that
    some reader could open is written whole. One that no reader can open, such
    as a screenshot, is written empty. That keeps nearly all the saving, because
    the bulk is pictures and video.
  - Links: a link to a file inside the repository is written as a copy of that
    file. Any other link is left out and named, with its true reason.
  - The proof changes. The oracle I chose, this repository's own cache, could
    not have caught either fault. The new test generates docs over the reduced
    tree and over the whole tree, on fixtures covering every language reader,
    and requires them to be identical.
  - Tests: the reviewer removed each of the round's three behaviours in turn and
    every test stayed green. The builder is adding a test repository that has
    excluded folders, bundle exclusions and links.

  The flaky clean-up test

  There is now a likelier cause than the one I gave you: two agents sharing
  /tmp. The reviewer lost scratch folders mid-run while the builder was tidying
  leftover folders by hand, and the builder's red runs found folders it had not
  made. If that is right, the product is sound and the test needs its own temp
  folder. The builder is proving it either way.

  Where that leaves the day

  The rebuild waits on these mends and a second review pass. The history panel
  case stays red until then. Everything else is unchanged: your packet and the
  December questions are the things waiting on you.

  ## Built: rule A, as commit `c9cbfb5d`

  Commit `c9cbfb5d` ("Read package scripts as a shell splits them, quoted
  command lines included") sits on top of `29aa50d0` on branch
  `worktree-agent-a83a6e42ef03d260e`. It can be dropped as a whole. I did not
  push or amend. It changes five files:
  - `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/json.ts`
  - `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/json.test.ts`
  - their two Live Docs
  - the probe README `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/
  Probes/script-entries/README.md`

  **What changed in `json.ts`.** Two functions, `filesRunBy` and
  `shellCommands`, replace the old split at `&&`, `||`, `;` and `|`
  (`COMMAND_SEPARATOR` and `runFileOf` are gone).
  - **Tokenizer:**
    - Quotes keep a word whole.
    - A `#` that begins a word starts a comment.
    - `&` separates commands, except inside `2>&1` and `&>`.
    - The file after `<`, `>`, `>>`, `&>`, `<<<` or `2>` is dropped from the
  command.
    - A backslash only escapes quotes, whitespace, `$`, a backtick and the
  operator characters, so `scripts\a.ts` stays a path.
    - `(` and `)` stay inside words.
  - **Rule:** in each command, the first file that is not a tooling input is
  what it runs. A quoted word that holds whitespace and names no file is read as
  a command line of its own. Today's filter is unchanged; there is no "code
  only" filter.
  - **Remarks in the code.** They cite the probe README and cover what you asked
  for:
    - A wrapper's command after a bare `--` is not read. They give your two
  examples, `dotenv -e config/dev.env -- vite-node scripts/a.ts` and the browser
  lane, and say what B would have found, what it would have cost (`-- go
  x.js`), and that it changed no real script (11 real scripts have a program
  name after `--`; in 7 it was an argument).
    - A quoted word after `-e` or `-c` is read like any other quoted word. That
  is right for a shell and only harmless by luck for inline JavaScript. The
  example is the engine's old `node -e "…copyFileSync(…)"` build; with `(` `)`
  split, its `dist/` path would map back to the emitter's source.
    - The other known wrong answers, and the never-right cases: `node
  scripts\a.ts` is read; `(cd …)`, `%VAR%` and `$(…)` are not.
  - The stale `--` remark the third review named was not in `json.ts` at
  `0bb8bbbc`.

  ## Tests: 11 red on the old code, all 72 green now

  The new block in `json.test.ts`, "JSON Adapter on package.json scripts, read
  as a shell reads them", has 49 tests: 36 rows that are right under A, 12 known
  wrong answers, and one guard. With the old file's 23, `json.test.ts` has 72.

  - `browserify-transform-cli … < ./lib/index.js > ./lib/browser.js`

  All 72 pass now.

  **Known wrong answers.** Each test is named "known wrong answer, pinned as it
  is: …". It asserts today's answer and also asserts that this answer differs
  from the right one. The 12 cases:
  - the two dotenv `--` forms and the browser lane
  - `--env-file .env`
  - a program after `-r`
  - `(cd x && node ../a.js)`
  - `%VAR%`
  - `$(…)` (as `node $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/scripts/a.js`; I replaced
  the measured `$(npm root)/..` row, which is only right by accident)

  - `cp src/a.js dist/`
  - `echo "node b.js must run first"`
  - `tee check.log`

  These pass on the old code and on A, because neither changes them.

  **Guard.** One test uses the engine's old copying build in a workspace whose
  `tsconfig` maps `dist/` to `src/`. It expects no entry. When I briefly split
  `(` and `)` as a shell does, this test went red, so it can fail.

› Message from general-purpose
  **Verdict: this would not have worked in front of the owner's colleagues today
  without preparation.** Generation finished on every corpus once I set the
  crashing files aside, and it fits comfortably in 8 GB: ordinary runs peaked
  between 0.1 and 1.5 GB. The trouble is the picture, which would not show what
  that audience expects. The three things most likely to embarrass us:

  1. **PowerShell comes out as dots with no wires.** In dbatools, 1,947 of 1,977
  files have no connection at all. The map only connects scripts through a
  dot-source, `Import-Module`, `using module` or `#requires` that writes the
  path out. A function defined in one script and called from another is never
  drawn: a seeded sample of 20 scripts had 64 such calls and the map drew none.
  `Invoke-Sqlcmd` and SQL inside PowerShell strings are not read. `inspect`
  answers "nothing" for every file, and `--reachable` calls all 1,821 `.ps1`
  files `Entry: program`, private function files included.
  2. **One apostrophe in a SQL name stops the whole run.** For example `[test
  that it doesn't fail]`. It also mis-numbers half of all SQL lines (details
  under "Facts by language").
  3. **Windows itself.** Live Docs checked out with CRLF line endings (Git for
  Windows' default) make `inspect` and `visualize` fail on the first doc. A
  config file saved by Windows PowerShell 5.1 (UTF-8 with a byte-order mark)
  stops every command. UTF-16 SQL and C# files, which SSMS writes, get no
  symbols and no warning.

  Close behind those:
  - `.ascx` user controls are never joined to their code-behind: 0 of 161 in
  DNN.
  - Designer and code-behind halves are linked in only 5 of 134 pairs.
  - 6% of PowerShell files have a parse error on common idioms, and in two of
  them the error swallowed the file's own public function.

  **The stranger's steps and stumbles.** I followed the README literally: clone,
  `npm ci`, `npm run build`, `live-docs:generate -- --workspace <folder>`,
  `live-docs:visualize -- --workspace <folder>`, then `inspect`.
  - **It writes a lot into the user's own repository.** `.live-documentation/`
  lands inside the mapped folder: 1,978 files for dbatools, 10,777 files (118
  MB) for DNN. Every PNG and GIF gets a doc, and nothing is git-ignored.
  - **"Current directory" means the tool's checkout.** Under `npm run`, the
  default and any relative `--workspace` are taken from the Live Documentation
  checkout, not from where the user stands.
  - **A second map overwrites the first.** `visualize` writes into this
  checkout's `dist/explorer` unless `--output` is given.
  - **A mistyped `--workspace` succeeds.** It reports "processed 0 files" and
  creates the misspelled folder.
  - **Crash messages don't help.** They name the generated Live Doc, not the
  source file, and give no stack. I had to write my own driver to get one and
  read `openings.ts`.
  - **A crash leaves half the output.** All docs are written but no index, and
  the next run calls those docs "unchanged".
  - **`inspect --from` is case-sensitive.** Backslash paths do work.
  - **Unread file types get empty docs silently.** `.SqlDataProvider` (DNN's 299
  SQL scripts), `.sqlproj`, `.sln`, `.vb` and `.bat` are not read, and
  generation doesn't say so.
  - **Where I had to read source to go on:** the SQL crash, why `.ascx` never
  links, which PowerShell connections are drawn at all, and why a class
  library's `ConfigurationManager` calls never reach `Web.config`.

  **Measurements.** Shared machine at load 7 to 18. Peak is the largest single
  process.

  | Corpus | Run | Wall s | Processor s | Peak memory |
  | --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | dbatools (1,977 docs) | default flags, run 1 / run 2 | 37.0 / 37.0 | 38.9 /
  38.8 | 877 / 878 MB |

  | DNN (10,776 docs) | default flags, through npm / direct | 176.2 / 142.4 |
  192.5 / 161.7 | 1,309 / 1,506 MB |
  | DNN | `--liftoff-only` | 158.1 | 179.9 | 1,412 MB |
  | DNN | visualize | 11.3 | 15.1 | 468 MB |

  Output sizes:
  - **dbatools:** 15 MB of docs; the Explorer is 13 MB.
  - **tSQLt:** 4.6 MB of docs; the Explorer is 9.7 MB.
  - **DNN:** 118 MB of docs; the Explorer is 46 MB, of which
  `explorer-data.json` is 37 MB.

  **For the mend's choice: `--liftoff-only` was slower on both real corpora.**
  On 1,790 real PowerShell scripts it took 43–46 s against 37 s, and about a
  fifth more processor time, while saving about 140 MB. DNN's C# went the same
  way, one run each. The optimizing tier pays for its 5–11 s compile on large
  corpora, so a mend should keep it and stop the main thread waiting, rather
  than turn it off. I did not test `--no-wasm-tier-up`.

  I couldn't explain the one 2.66 GB peak.

  **Every crash** (I had to change no product code to get past them):
  1. **SQL bracketed name containing an apostrophe.** Smallest input, three
  lines: `CREATE PROC [a'b]` / `'` / `x]`.
     - Message: `….sql.md: line 7: unexpected evidence on the opening entry …`.
  A variant reads `expected "", found "- Entry: opening …\r"`.
     - Stack: `graphFiles.ts:36` ← `readLiveDocGraph` (`graphFiles.ts:29`) ←
  `generateLiveDocs` (`generator.ts:292`).
     - Fault: `stripSql` in `packages/engine/src/live-docs/openings.ts:293`
  doesn't skip `[…]` identifiers, so the apostrophe opens a string and the name
  runs across lines into the rendered doc.
     - Five tSQLt test files crash this way; I excluded them through the config.
  46 more names come out garbled without crashing.
  2. **A `)` in a linked path.** `Lib - Copy (2).ps1` plus a script that
  dot-sources it gives `unexpected dependency line "- [`Lib - Copy (2)`](./Lib -
  Copy (2).ps1.md)"`. The reader's link pattern `\(([^)]+)\)` (`document.ts`
  lines 421, 631, 653, 672, 682) stops at the `)`.
  3. **Config file with a byte-order mark.** Message: `Unexpected token '' … is
  not valid JSON`. Fault: `scripts/live-docs/cli.ts:68` doesn't strip the BOM.

  **Facts by language.** "Resolved" means a dependency points at a file in the
  map; "isolated" means no link in or out.
  - **dbatools `.ps1`:** 46% have no symbols (the tests); 1% of links resolve;
  99% isolated.
  - **tSQLt `.sql`:** 21% no symbols, 66% resolved, 25% isolated.
  - **DNN `.cs`:** 2% no symbols, 95% resolved, 6% isolated.
  - **DNN `.csproj`:** every `ProjectReference` resolves.
  - **DNN `.config`:** 94% get no symbols; only files with `appSettings` or
  `connectionStrings` get any.
  - **DNN markup:** `.aspx` 100% resolved; `.ascx` and `.cshtml` 100% isolated.

  Twenty-file samples (seed 1119):
  - **PowerShell (dbatools).**
    - Right: functions and their help, 25 of 25.
    - Missing: all 64 cross-script calls.
    - Wrong: every function file is labelled `program`; across the whole corpus,
  17 files' symbols differ from the source.
  - **SQL (tSQLt).**
    - Right: every top-level object; `EXEC` of another procedure resolved in 23
  of 28 cases.
    - Missing: scalar function calls inside expressions (e.g.
  `tSQLt.Private_IsTestClass(...)`), and objects created inside `EXEC('CREATE
  …')`.
    - Wrong: 827 of 1,620 symbols point at the wrong line after any comment or
  string spanning several lines. Smallest case: a 3-line `/* */` before `CREATE
  TABLE` gives `#L2` instead of `#L4`. System catalog names (`sys.*`) appear as
  dependencies, as do `BIGINT` and CTE names.
  - **C# (DNN).**
    - Right: types 24 of 25; 65 of 71 links go to a file that declares a type
  the source names.
    - Missing: designer/code-behind links. In the small owner-shaped project I
  built, `new SqlCommand("dbo.usp_GetCustomer")` is not linked to the procedure,
  and a class library's `ConfigurationManager` calls don't reach the web
  project's `Web.config`.
    - Wrong: nothing found.
  - **Config and project files.** All 48 sampled project references link, in
  classic and SDK-style projects alike. Six of seven other configs have empty
  docs.

  Which connections the map draws:
  - **Drawn:**
    - dot-source with a written path (backslashes included) and `Import-Module`
  with a path
    - `ProjectReference`
    - `.aspx` → code-behind
    - procedure `EXEC` procedure, and SQL naming a table
    - C# inline SQL naming a table
    - WCF endpoints as symbols
  - **Not drawn:**
    - cross-script PowerShell function calls, and dot-sourcing in a loop
    - a `.psd1`'s `FunctionsToExport` → the files defining them
    - `Invoke-Sqlcmd`
    - `.sln` → projects
    - `.ascx` → code-behind, and `Register Src`
    - partial halves to each other
    - C# naming a stored procedure
    - a library → its host's config
    - SQL scalar function calls
  - The probe README has an example of each from the corpora.

  **Windows.**
  - **Tested here by imitation:**
    - Works: UTF-16 and CRLF PowerShell, UTF-8-with-BOM PowerShell, backslash
  dot-source paths, backslash `--from`.
    - Fails: CRLF Live Docs break `inspect` and `visualize`, and `--check`
  reports every doc as changed. Regenerating does keep the authored Purpose.
  UTF-16 SQL and C# give no symbols. A BOM in the config and a `)` in a file
  name both crash.
  - **Grammar errors.** 116 of 1,836 PowerShell files have parse errors.
  One-line repros:
    - `$b * 1MB`
    - `-cnotin`
    - `continue main` (a labelled loop)
    - `switch ($t) { "A B" { 1 } }` (a double-quoted label containing a space)
    - `switch -regex` with `"^(A|B)$"`
    - `[Outer+Inner]::Value`
    - `-Value $o.GetType().Name` (method call then member, as a command
  argument)
    - `A, B , C` (space before a comma)
    - a backtick line continuation inside a method call
  - **Not tested:** a real Windows run, cmd.exe, NTFS, an 8 GB laptop.
  - **Suspected, from a read-only review** by one subagent (general-purpose,
  code review only):
    - Case-insensitive probes keep the spelling they asked for (`Web.config` vs
  `web.config`, project references, code-behind), so links are lost on Windows
  that work on Linux.
    - If git fails (an ownership check on a network share, or git not on PATH),
  ignore rules quietly fall back to the root `.gitignore`, and `bin/` and `obj/`
  get documented.
    - `exclude` matches case-sensitively.
    - Long paths.
    - Byte-wise sort order differs between `\` and `/`, which can make Windows
  and Linux write different docs.
    - Files saved in Windows-1252 (ANSI) are read as UTF-8 without a warning.

  **What I did not get to:**
  - the browser
  - Windows itself
  - the cause of the 2.66 GB peak
  - a phase-by-phase breakdown of where generation's time goes on these corpora
  - `--no-wasm-tier-up`
  - tSQLt's PowerShell and DNN's JavaScript beyond the counts
  - `live-docs:history`

  The probe is written up in `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/a
  gent-a85e3b36f033509fc/AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-
  19/Probes/cold-run/README.md` and committed as `9b2fcd4c` on branch
  `worktree-agent-a85e3b36f033509fc`; it is not pushed. No other file changed.
  The page opens with its title heading and then "Current as of November 19,
  2026.", as the other probe pages do.

● The tool has now been run cold on public code shaped like your work's, and the
  verdict is that it would not have worked in front of your colleagues today
  without preparation. It runs and it fits in memory, but the map it draws of
  that kind of code is not what they'd expect. The full write-up is here
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/cold-run/REA
  DME.md).

  The three corpora were a repository of 1,790 Windows PowerShell scripts, a
  T-SQL codebase, and a large .NET Framework platform.

  What works

  - It finishes and fits in 8 GB. Once three crashing files were set aside,
    generation ran on all three and peaked between 0.1 and 1.5 GB. The largest,
    10,776 files, took about three minutes.
  - C# is in good shape. 95% of its references resolve, every project reference
    resolves, and .aspx pages reach their code-behind.

  What would have embarrassed us

  Area: PowerShell
  What happens: Scripts come out as dots with no wires. A function defined in
    one script and called from another is never drawn.
  How bad: 1,947 of 1,977 files have no connection at all
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: PowerShell
  What happens: Every .ps1 is labelled a program, including files that only
    define functions
  How bad: "What is reachable from an entry" says everything
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: SQL
  What happens: One apostrophe inside a bracketed name, such as [test that it
    doesn't fail], stops the whole run
  How bad: 5 files in that codebase
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: SQL
  What happens: Symbols point at the wrong line after any multi-line comment
  How bad: 827 of 1,620 symbols
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Windows
  What happens: Live Docs checked out with Windows line endings can't be read;
    a config file saved by PowerShell 5.1 stops every command; UTF-16 SQL and
    C# files get no symbols and no warning
  How bad: Each is a default on Windows
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: .NET
  What happens: .ascx user controls never reach their code-behind; a stored
    procedure named from C# isn't linked to it
  How bad: 0 of 161 controls
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Command line
  What happens: A relative --workspace is taken from the tool's folder, not
    yours; a mistyped one "succeeds"; a second map overwrites the first
  How bad: Met in the first ten minutes

  What I've started

  Three builders are out, each on its own branch:

  1. The SQL reader. The crash, the line numbers and the wrong dependencies,
     graded against Microsoft's own T-SQL parser so the reader isn't marking its
     own work.
  2. PowerShell connections. Measured first, built second. PowerShell finds a
     function by name at run time, so any rule is a guess about names. The
     builder scores candidate rules against PowerShell's own parser on real
     scripts and brings me the table before building anything.
  3. The command line and Windows text. Each stumble above, with a test that
     fails first.

  Not yet sent: the .NET gaps, and nine common PowerShell idioms the grammar
  fails to parse. I'll send those as reviews come off my desk.

  None of this lands alone. It is all code the history cache is keyed on, so it
  gathers for a landing after the engine round's.

  What I need from you

  1. Which of these shapes does your work's code have most of? For example:
     scripts calling each other's functions, scripts running SQL, user controls,
     or procedures named from C#. Nothing waits on this, since every item above
     is a fault whoever's code it is, but it sets the order.
  2. Should the tool write its docs inside the repository it maps? Today it
     does, with nothing git-ignored: 10,777 files and 118 MB for the largest
     corpus.
  3. Should every picture file get a doc of its own? Today each one does.

  One thing bears on the PowerShell wait from earlier: on the real scripts,
  switching V8's optimizer off was slower (43–46 seconds against 37). So that
  fix can't be "turn it off"; the builder has the numbers.

  ## Second leg, map-reds: the placement solver is faster with every picture the
  same, Settle Hold measured, and both 2D cases still red

  **Branch** `worktree-agent-a4bcc8d5e1325a566`, tip `0968ca60`, four commits on
  `01b32fb0`. Nothing pushed. No bound, wait, tag or default changed.

  ### Commits
  - `c4fb0f68` **Step 1, incremental exchange.** After an exchange, only the
  subtree under the entering edge's common ancestor is renumbered, and its sums
  and cut values are worked out in the same walk (Gansner's update, as Graphviz
  does it). The numbers come out exactly as a full rebuild gives them. This
  commit also adds the equivalence harness,
  `network-simplex.equivalence.test.ts`, which keeps the solver as of `01b32fb0`
  inside the test only, as its oracle. It also adds a fixture of three real
  systems, `network-simplex.systems.json` (115 KB), and the Live Docs for both
  new files.
  - `38225b30` **Step 3a, tight tree.** The tight tree now grows from two heaps
  of the edges leaving it (one by tail, one by head), ordered by slack and then
  by edge number, with the tree's shift kept as one running number. Before,
  every edge was read and every node moved each time the tree grew.
  - `a83a89fb` **Step 3b, entering edge.** The entering edge is now looked for
  only among edges of the nodes below the leaving edge, as Graphviz's
  `enter_edge` does; ties still go to the lowest edge number. The head side is
  raised from the same range, keeping the old rule that nodes in another tree of
  the forest count as head side.
  - `0968ca60` Evidence: `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11
  -19/Probes/map-reds/README.md`, section "Second leg".

  The files changed are `network-simplex.ts`, its new test and fixture, and
  their Live Docs. No placement or measuring module was changed.

  ### Equivalence proof, for each step
  - **Harness:** seeded random systems of 2 to 300 nodes (many ties, forests of
  several trees) plus the three real systems give identical JSON to the oracle.
    - I checked the harness can fail: with ties broken the other way, both of
  its cases fail.
    - All 466 2D unit cases pass. Lint, type-check and the markdown link check
  are clean, and the Live Docs are regenerated.
  - **All 342 real systems:** I made a scratch build of the client whose solver
  recorded every system it was given (not committed). The page solved 342
  systems during the five-file, 80-seed search, and the search ended exactly as
  on `main`. Each step gives the same JSON as the oracle on all 342.
  - **On the page:** each step's build ended the 80-seed search exactly as
  today's does: 80 seeds, 231 joins, 29 adoptions (26 of them joins), seed 77,
  price 3,197,457.
