  The single file

  A builder bundled our generator, unchanged, into one page and ran it in
  Chromium:

  - It writes what Node writes. On the estate sample the page produced the same
    39 docs as the command line, line for line apart from the timestamp.
  - Weight: 5.6 MB to scan, about 7.2 MB with the viewer, plus the map's data.
    The TypeScript compiler is 3.6 MB of that and could be swapped out.
  - Speed and memory: 1,973 PowerShell scripts took 62 seconds in a tab that
    peaked at 608 MB. Node took 60 to 82 seconds on the same machine.
  - From a file opened off disk, the folder picker is available, and the page
    downloaded a blank copy of itself that started without error.
  - It fetches nothing: the page's security policy allows no network requests at
    all.

  What a page cannot do is anything that runs git. That means history, and the
  full rules for ignored files. History would stay a command-line step, possibly
  one that writes its result into the file.

  Not proven: the real folder prompt, because automation cannot press it. For
  comparison, the command line packaged without our checkout installs at 100 MB
  and then fails to build the Explorer.

  My recommendation: make this file the product's main way in, as the next large
  build after the engine round and the readers for your code. I can also have
  the test page rebuilt for you to open on your work machine. It reads a folder
  you pick, writes nothing, and shows time and memory, which would settle the
  two things only that machine can tell us. Do you want that?

  Trial pictures for the packet

  - 2D alternatives (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/S
    creenshots/owner-packet/2d-alternatives/README.md). My rule for the history
    bar's mark was wrong. Of four marks built, the best keeps the thumb but
    stands it outside the bar past a break, with its own date under it. Testers
    still read the gap as empty, so it gets one more pass before becoming the
    default.
  - 3D alternatives (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/S
    creenshots/owner-packet/3d-alternatives/README.md). With "touch, then rest",
    the 2D map holds still under a moving pointer; today 21 of 31 changes span
    the whole map. Opening tests in 3D costs nothing on your graphics card and
    is unusable without one, so the limit stays until you can try the setting on
    your work machine.

  Node 26

  It works: every doc and every oracle comparison is byte-identical, and this
  repository generates in about half the time. One Explorer unit test fails only
  on Node 26, in the last digit of a floating-point result, and is being fixed.
  Node 24 is ruled out because one compile takes up to 1.5 GB.

  Found tonight that would have hit your code

  - PowerShell files named Test-Something are labelled as tests and would be
    hidden by "Show Tests". Being fixed.
  - Generation crashes on a large .NET codebase (about 4,000 C# files) with a
    stack overflow in the doc writer. Being fixed.
  - The C# reader slows sharply as files grow: 400 files take 15 seconds, 2,000
    take over four minutes. Being fixed.
  - OPENQUERY to Oracle already connects to the Oracle side's script, but only
    while a schema and table name are unique on the board.

  One question

  To make the Oracle link certain, the board needs one line per linked server
  saying which piece it reaches. Would you rather write that on the SQL Server
  piece ("linked server ORACLE_CENTRAL is oracle") or on the Oracle piece
  ("reached as ORACLE_CENTRAL")?

  Every timing tonight is suspect: a dozen builders have the machine at a load
  of 20 to 40. When this wave hands back I'll hold new work and run the timing
  measurements on a quiet machine.

  ## Fourth leg: handback

  Four commits on `worktree-agent-ab441e69edee8fd9c`, on top of `5225ffd4`. None
  was amended or pushed. The working tree is clean, and `--check` reports 1,092
  docs unchanged after the last commit.

  **Not done: the CommonMark oracle is not committed.** It shows that my flat
  escape of docstrings cannot contain blocks inside list items. That is
  unfinished work, below.

  ### The commits
  1. **`26fc4dcd`** Write a quoted name inside its code span, and a doc's title
  and Code Path as names *(rulings 1 and 2)*.
     - `NAME` now writes its quoted form as `codeSpan(quote(value))`. A name
  beginning with `"` is quoted, and the reader decides by the first character
  inside the span.
     - The title and the `Code Path` are written as names: `` # `src/a.ts` ``.
     - `TEXT` is gone. `LINK` and `SOURCE` keep their bare JSON form, which only
  a lone surrogate reaches.
     - The tests' hand-written docs take the new forms: `generator.test.ts`'s
  two orphan docs, and the `document.test` and `compose.test` expectations.
     - This commit regenerates every doc of this repository.
  2. **`a2c7bc14`** Contain a docstring by CommonMark's own block rules *(ruling
  3)*.
     - Fences are read by CommonMark's rule: backticks or tildes, up to three
  spaces of indent, and a closer at least as long as its opener. The writer and
  the reader use the same rule.
     - A docstring line is written behind a backslash when it opens either of
  these and nothing later in the docstring closes it:
       - a fence;
       - an HTML block that only a closer ends: a comment, `<script>`, `<pre>`,
  `<style>`, `<textarea>`, `<?`, `<!X` or `<![CDATA[`.
     - Inside such an HTML block, fence lines and heading lines are escaped for
  our reader's sake.
     - What closes later is counted once from the end, so the escape stays
  linear.
     - Red first: a new grammar test with an unclosed comment, `<script>`, tilde
  fence, and a four-backtick fence closed only by three. The committed writer
  wrote the comment and the script bare.
     - One doc moved: `document.ts`'s own.
  3. **`3e3add72`** Describe a link-shaped key in words.
     - The docs lint takes `[a](https://…)` anywhere as an absolute link, code
  spans included, so it failed the gate on my own note. It also gave a false
  failure on the decision.
  4. **`1dd746a1`** Append a doc's lines one by one *(your Dnn.Platform crash)*.
     - **Cause:** a spread, not recursion. `renderLiveDoc` spread the authored
  block's lines into one `push`, as did `pushSection` (a section's lines) and
  `renderSymbolBlocks` (a docstring's lines). The engine refuses about 130,000
  arguments; I measured 125,000 ok and 150,000 throws.
     - **Mend:** an `append` loop in place of all three spreads. The composer's
  one spread (an example's description) is also a loop now.
     - **Reader:** the reader had no such spread.
     - **Red first:** a doc of 200,000 lines in its authored block, one
  docstring and its dependencies threw at line 452 (the authored spread). Now it
  writes and reads back. Writing and reading are linear: 100,000 lines take
  about 0.5 s and 0.35 s.
     - **The test has its own 60 s timeout:** it bounds size, not speed, and
  took 6.5 s at load average 36.
     - **Corpus:** I did not run `/home/node/scratch-dotnet-gaps/`.

  ### What moved in this repository
  - `26fc4dcd` regenerated all 1,092 docs. 1,091 changed in exactly three lines:
  the title, the `Code Path`, and **`Generated At`**.
  - You expected two lines. The third is `Generated At`: the generator stamps a
  new timestamp on any doc whose text changes, so it cannot be avoided without
  changing that rule.
  - The 1,092nd, `document.ts`'s own doc, also moved its `Source` line numbers.
  - Every later commit moved only the docs of the files it edits. `--check` was
  green after each commit.

  ### What the reference parser found (`commonmark` 0.31.2)
  1. **A quoted name was not inert.** Written bare, `"<img src=x>\n"` was raw
  HTML, and a key holding a markdown link became a live link. **Mended** in
  `26fc4dcd`.
  2. **Raw titles were markup.** **Mended** in `26fc4dcd`.
     - 8 docs here showed `__init__.py` as a bold "init".
     - A file named `<b>x</b>.ts` gave HTML; one named `[a](b).ts` gave a link.
  3. **Docstring blocks that run on.** Partly mended in `a2c7bc14`.
     - An unclosed HTML comment ran until the next LIVE-DOC marker's `-->`.
     - An unclosed `<script>`/`<pre>`/`<style>`/`<textarea>`, a `<?` or
  `<![CDATA[` behaves the same way.
     - So does an unclosed tilde fence, or a fence closed by a shorter run.
     - **One rule I had wrong at first:** CommonMark closes any of the four
  type-1 blocks (`<script>`, `<pre>`, `<style>`, `<textarea>`) with any of their
  four end tags, so `</pre>` closes a `<script>`. The writer now follows that
  rule.
  4. **Not mended: containers.** The fuzz oracle found it on `" éB\n<!--\n
  ```"` in a Links section.
     - The section's items are `- …` list items. A fence opened inside a list
  item ends when the item ends, at the next `- ` line. My flat simulation
  instead paired it with a fence line further down.
     - Then a `<!--` at column 0, which the writer took to be inside that fence,
  opened an HTML block. It ran over the rest of the doc, up to the end marker's
  `-->`.
     - Containing that means modelling CommonMark's containers (list items,
  block quotes) in the writer. A more conservative alternative: never let a
  docstring line open one of those HTML blocks, or a fence, unless it closes on
  the same line or the next lines close it at the same container depth.
     - This is a design choice I could not finish within the time. The oracle
  stays red on it.
  5. **Setext underlines and link reference definitions** can't capture the
  format's own lines. A blank line always separates a docstring from the next
  structural line, and every generated link is inline.

  past its docstring" check, and no image or raw HTML outside docstrings;

  - **What it treats specially:**
    - a quoted (lone-surrogate) link is exempt;
    - a block may end on the blank line after its body;
    - docs over 200,000 characters are left to our reader alone, since the

  ## 1. Linear reading (`95fb8cf9`)

  One 80 KB paragraph, timed with the same script before and after:

  | Hostile paragraph | Before: read / rendered (load 27) | After: read /
  rendered (load 31) |
  |---|---|---|
  | Run of unclosed `[a](` | 69.5 s / 81.6 s | 0.28 s / 0.23 s |
  | Unclosed `<!-- ` | 3.9 s / 4.6 s | 0.10 s / 0.11 s |
  | Unclosed `<? ` | 5.3 s / 5.0 s | 0.12 s / 0.10 s |
  | Unclosed `<!a ` | 10.6 s / 12.5 s | 0.14 s / 0.08 s |
  | Many images | 9.9 s / 2.1 s | 0.09 s / 0.22 s |
  | Unmatched `[` then links | 5.8 s / 8.7 s | 0.08 s / 0.18 s |
  | 20,000 links rendered | 11.4 s | 0.30 s |

  How:
  - **Parenthesis nesting.** A bare destination's parentheses nest at most 32
  deep. cmark sets the same bound, and the specification explicitly allows one.
  With it, any character is scanned by at most about 33 attempts.
  - **Comments, instructions, CDATA and declarations.** Each ends at a closer,
  found by one search that reading reuses as it moves forward. No regular
  expression is used for these any more.
  - **Openers.** Making a link deactivates the earlier openers by one number,
  not a walk.
  - **Code spans.** A code span's closer comes from an index of the paragraph's
  backtick runs.
  - **Images.** One sweep finds what stands inside an image.
  - **Renderer.** It starts each link's text at its first token by a binary
  search.

  The proof counts steps rather than time. The reader exposes `readingSteps` and
  the renderer `renderingSteps`, and each tests the same rule: ten times the
  text must cost fewer than twelve times the steps.
  - The reader has ten hostile cases. Lifting the nesting bound, the closer
  reuse and the one-number deactivation makes seven of them fail.
  - The renderer has three cases. Removing its start index makes all three fail.
  - **Limit:** the count does not see scanning inside a regular expression. The
  patterns left (tags, autolinks, angle destinations, labels) are each bounded
  by their own delimiters.

  Five commits sit on my branch on top of `17532f56` (`ab9e346d6`, `0d20cc89c`,

  **Verification.** `npm run safe:commit` without the browser passed on its last
  run, at a load average of 28 to 34:
  - Live Docs: 1,099 docs, all unchanged; lint, build and test type-check
  passed.
  - Unit tests: 203 files, 2,298 tests. Integration tests: 21 files, 170 tests.
  - Link enforcement and the three SlopCop audits passed, and the tree was clean
  afterwards.

  The run before it (before item 5) had two load timeouts at a load average of
  31 to 33: `generatorIdentity.test.ts` at 5.8 s and `forceGraphOctree.test.ts`
  at 6.0 s. Both passed on the next run. The browser stage was not run; this leg
  changes no Explorer file.

  **1. One reading step (`ab9e346d6`).**
  `packages/engine/src/tooling/sourceText.ts` provides `decodeSourceText(bytes)`
  and `readSourceText(path)`. The rule:
  - A byte-order mark decides first: UTF-8, UTF-16 LE or UTF-16 BE. The mark is
  not part of the text.
  - UTF-16 without a mark is accepted only when it is certain: an even number of
  bytes, where every pair is one zero byte and one non-zero byte, the zero
  always on the same side. That means every character is between U+0001 and
  U+00FF.
    - **What I refuse to guess:** unmarked UTF-16 containing any character above
  U+00FF.
  - Next, valid UTF-8 with no zero byte is read as UTF-8.
  - Then Windows-1252, if there is no zero byte and no control character other
  than tab, LF, VT, FF, CR or 0x1A.
  - Anything else is not read: the reader gets empty text and the file is named.

  Tests:
  - Real bytes for each case, checked against PowerShell 7.7's `Get-Content -Raw
  -Encoding …` (utf8NoBOM, utf8, Unicode, BigEndianUnicode, 1252). All match
  code unit for code unit. These checks are skipped when `pwsh` is absent.
  - I moved 16 readers onto the step: the TypeScript reader, the symbol-index
  pass, the re-export follower, the DOM heuristic, and the Java, Go/go.mod,
  Rust/Cargo, C/C++, Python, Ruby, JSON, HTML, CSS, pom and TOML adapters.
  - `sourceEncodings.test.ts` saves one file per language as UTF-16 LE and BE
  (with marks) and as Windows-1252, and holds each to its UTF-8 twin. All 21
  cases failed before the change.
  - `json.ts:166` reads tsconfig through TypeScript's own `ts.sys.readFile`,
  which already decodes by the mark, so I left it.
  - The history's tree writer writes blobs with nothing converted (`cat-file
  --batch`; I read `history/git.ts`), so it needs no change.

  `generate` now says which rule read the files, for example `[live-docs] 1 file
  read as Windows-1252, since it is not UTF-8: src/ansi.py.`, a matching line
  for UTF-16, and on stderr the names of files that are not text, with the
  reason. To count this, the generator reads each such file's bytes a second
  time.

  **Simplest correct form:** the generator should read each file's bytes once
  and pass the decoded text through `analyzeSourceFile` and
  `analyzeWithLanguageAdapters` as `content`, so no adapter opens its own file.
  That changes those two signatures, `buildWorkspaceIndexes`,
  `LanguageAdapter.analyze`'s input, and the first line of each adapter. I did
  not change any signature.

  **Swaps for whoever joins the branches.** Add `import { readSourceText } from
  "../../tooling/sourceText";` and drop `fs` where it becomes unused:
  - `adapters/sql.ts:27`: `const content  = await fs.readFile(absolutePath,
  "utf8");` → `const content  = await readSourceText(absolutePath);`
  - `adapters/aspnet.ts:18`: `const content = await fs.readFile(absolutePath,
  "utf8");` → `const content = await readSourceText(absolutePath);`
  - `adapters/csharp.ts:893`: `const content = await fs.readFile(absolutePath,
  "utf8");` → `const content = await readSourceText(absolutePath);`
  - `adapters/csharp.ts:1249`: `const content  = await fs.readFile(absolutePath,
  "utf8");` → `const content  = await readSourceText(absolutePath);`
  - `adapters/dotnetConfig.ts:75`: `const content = await
  fs.readFile(absolutePath, "utf8");` → `const content = await
  readSourceText(absolutePath);`
  - `adapters/project.ts:58`: `const content  = await fs.readFile(absolutePath,
  "utf8");` → `const content  = await readSourceText(absolutePath);`
  - `adapters/powershell.ts:934`: `const content = decodeScript(await
  fs.readFile(absolutePath));` → `const content = await
  readSourceText(absolutePath);` After this, `decodeScript` (`:902–906`) has no
  caller left and can be deleted.

  `openings.ts` and `csharp.dependencies.ts` open no file. `dotnetPaths.ts` and
  `solution.ts` are not on my branch.

  **2. The generator finishes a run that meets a file it cannot map
  (`0d20cc89c`).**
  - Each file's work, from the binary sniff to the write, is now in one `try`.
  Each doc is read back with `parseLiveDoc` before it is written.
  - A file that cannot be read, or whose doc the reader refuses, goes into
  `failed` with its source path and the reason. The run still writes every other
  doc and the index.
  - `generate` then prints "Could not map N file(s)…", one indented `path:
  reason` line per file, a pointer to `exclude`, and exits 1.
  - A failed file's old doc is removed only if the reader refuses it and it
  holds no authored words.
  - Test: `generator.unmappable.test.ts` wraps the writer to produce a refused
  doc for one file of three, plus a file that cannot be opened (skipped on
  Windows and as root). Both failed before the change.

  What I changed in `generator.ts`. None of it touches the ordering near line
  137 or `classifyChange`.
  - **Imports:** `hasReader`, and `decodeSourceText, type SourceTextReading`.
  - **Result:** the new fields `failed`, `readings` and `notices`, the
  `SourceReadings` type, and the three collections declared beside `results`.
  - **In the loop:**
    - `try {` before the binary sniff;
    - `if (hasReader(...)) noteReading(...)`;
    - the notices loop after `analyzeSourceFile`;
    - the `parseLiveDoc(rendered)` read-back before `results.push`;
    - `} catch (error) { failed.push(...); await removeRefusedDoc(...) }` after
  the write.
  - **New functions:** `noteReading` and `removeRefusedDoc`, after
  `generateLiveDocs`.
  - **Indentation:** the loop body inside the `try` keeps its old indentation,
  to keep the join small. Please indent it at the join.

  **3. `exclude` and filename case (`e05c5d948`).** `gitIgnoresCase` asks `git
  config --type=bool --get core.ignorecase`. `matchesPathPattern` gains an
  `ignoreCase` flag, and discovery passes it for `exclude` only. Entry points
  and archetype overrides still match exactly. Tests with `core.ignorecase` set
  true, set false, and absent, using `vendor/**` against `Vendor/Lib.cs`; the
  "true" case failed first.

  **4. Refusals printed whole.** By reading the code, not by a run (that message
  lives on the other branch):
  - `runCommand` prints `error.message` whole;
  - `--stack` prints the whole stack, whose first line is the message;
  - a `UsageError` prints its message whole before the `--help` pointer;
  - my new failure lines print each reason whole.

  So the names branch's message, including its last sentence, comes through
  intact.

  **5. Notices channel (`b0f634882`).**
  - `coreTypes.ts` gains `notices?: SourceNotice[]` (`{ reason, subject }`);
  `analyzeSourceFile` passes an adapter's result through whole.
  - The generator counts notices by reason in first-seen order, each subject as
  `<source path>: <subject>`.
  - `generate` prints one line per reason with its count, for example
  `[live-docs] paths several projects hold, left unlinked: 149.`, and a new
  `--verbose` lists the subjects. Nothing goes into a Live Doc.
  - Test: a fake reader giving two reasons across three of four files; it failed
  first. The command's printed line itself has no test, because a fake reader
  can't be injected into the command's process.

  The one line each reader would add:
  - **.NET:** `notices.push({ reason: "paths several projects hold, left
  unlinked", subject: specifier });`
  - **SQL:** ``notices.push({ reason: "SQL strings computed in part, not read",
  subject: `line ${line}` });``
  - **PowerShell:** ``notices.push({ reason: "loads whose path the text does not
  fix, not followed", subject: `line ${line}: ${command}` });``

  ## The history's key: it changes
  `generatorIdentity.ts` hashes the committed blob of `package-lock.json` into
  `code`:
  - `checkoutOf()` runs `git ls-tree HEAD -- package-lock.json` and folds that
  into the hash (`.update("tool-lock\0").update(lockEntry …)`).
  - `lens.ts` `lensOf()` keys on `{version, code, modified, unicode}` plus the
  configuration.

  So the new lock gives a new key and the history rebuilds from scratch.
  Deleting `packages/cli` by itself would not change the key, since only
  `packages/engine`, `packages/generator` and `tsconfig.base.json` are keyed.
  But the lock has to change when a workspace is removed.

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Third leg: PL/SQL graded against an independent parser, two Oracle mends,
  and the kinds counted

  The leg is done: the grading of today's reader on PL/SQL, the mend of what
  broke reading, and the count of the kinds of symbol, measured and not built.
  Two commits on the branch, on top of `c07205b0`; none amended, none pushed.
  The probe README is brought level under "The third leg".
  - `32578989`: Read past Oracle's q-quoted strings and SQL*Plus PROMPT and REM
  lines.
  - `5552afe6`: the record.

  ### The independent parser, and its limits
  - **Parser.** The ANTLR project's PL/SQL grammar (grammars-v4 `sql/plsql`,
  master as of today), generated with the ANTLR 4.13.2 tool jar and compiled
  with the Java 21 already on the machine. Everything was fetched and built in
  scratch; nothing was installed machine-wide.
  - **Emitter.** A small Java program walks the parse tree and records top-level
  creates, the members of packages, tables read or written, call statements and
  calls with arguments, each with its line. Neither the emitter nor the
  generated parser is committed; the emitter is about 150 lines and can go under
  `scripts/oracle/` if you want a kept record.
  - **What it cannot judge, said plainly in the record:**
    - **It does not resolve names.** It cannot tell a package member from a
  schema function, `arr(i)` from a call, or a local procedure from a standalone
  one. So only calls written with two or more parts are graded; one-part calls
  are counted.
    - **It refuses 98 of utPLSQL's 366 files.** All 17 synonyms fail on `create
  noneditionable synonym`; type attributes written `integer default` and `(self
  as …)` also fail, as do several core package bodies.
    - **It does not read SQL*Plus.** Both readers got the same text, with all
  693 SQL*Plus lines blanked and their line numbers kept. `EXEC` lines were
  among them, so their calls are on neither side.
  - **Codebase.** utPLSQL, the develop branch. Its `.pks`, `.pkb`, `.tps`,
  `.tpb`, `.syn` and `.trg` files were given `.sql` names, since the reader
  reads only `.sql`.

  ### Today's reader on utPLSQL (268 files the grammar parses)

  | Kind | Objects | Found by the reader |
  |---|---|---|
  | Package specs | 40 (26 bodies, 976 members) | 0 |
  | Type specs | 113 (55 bodies) | 0 |
  | Tables | 15 | 11, all 11 at the right line |
  | Sequences | 4 | 0 |
  | Standalone procedures | 1 | 1, right line |
  | Standalone functions | 1 | 1, right line |

  - **Objects invented:** none. Why the reader misses four of the tables was not
  looked into.
  - **Dependency lines:** 433 expected. Before the mend 266 were right, 167
  missing and 243 invented; after it, 270, 163 and 248.
  - **Most of the misses are package calls.** A call into a package should be a
  link to the package's spec file; today it is kept as a name (`= ut.run`), so
  each counts once as missing and once as invented.
  - **Counted but not graded:** 973 one-part calls and 88 one-part call
  statements, plus 33 other places a table is named.

  ### The mend, red first (`32578989`)
  - **The q-quoted string.** `q'[it's]'` (in 18 utPLSQL files) is now read as
  one string. That covers `q`, `Q`, `nq`, `NQ`, any delimiter, and brackets that
  close with their pair. Before, its apostrophe opened a string and everything
  after it was misread.
  - **`PROMPT`, `PRO`, `REM` and `REMARK` lines.** These are now skipped to the
  end of the line, but only where a statement could begin: first on its line,
  after a `;`, a `/`, a `GO`, or at the start of the text. A column named
  `Remark` on its own line inside a query stays a name, and the test holds that.
  utPLSQL has no such line with an apostrophe; my owner-shaped trial in the
  first leg had one.
  - **Effect.** One unit test was red before and is green. On utPLSQL, 4 more
  dependency lines are right, and 5 package calls the q-quotes had hidden now
  appear as kept names. tSQLt was re-graded with ScriptDom: 969 of 969,
  unchanged.
  - **Nothing else breaks reading.** On the raw text (SQL*Plus lines left in)
  the reader loses nothing; it has 12 more invented lines, which are the calls
  on the `EXEC` lines the split had blanked.

  ### Kinds of symbol: measured, not built
  How many of each kind the corpora hold, and how many lines kept by name today
  would become links if the kind were a symbol:

  | Corpus | Kind | Count | Kept names that would become links |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | utPLSQL | packages | 40 specs, 976 members | 212 (221 on the raw text) |
  | utPLSQL | types | 113 | 1 |
  | utPLSQL | sequences | 4 | 0, since `NEXTVAL` is not read as a use |
  | utPLSQL | synonyms | 17 files | not measured: the grammar refuses them |
  | utPLSQL | triggers | 1 file | not measured: the grammar refuses it |
  | tSQLt | types | 9 | 0 |
  | tSQLt | triggers | 1 | 0 |
  | tSQLt | synonyms | 3, all inside test bodies | 0 |
  | DNN | types | 4 | 0 |

  - **Packages are the kind that matters.** On a package-built codebase, 212 of
  the 433 expected dependency lines are calls into a package, and today none of
  them is drawn as a wire. On utPLSQL nearly all code lives in packages: 2
  standalone routines against 976 package members. That is the number to put to
  the owner.
  - **The other kinds matter only once their uses are read**, which is a
  separate reading step: types of parameters and columns, a trigger's table,
  `NEXTVAL`, a synonym's target.
  - **What a new kind touches:**
    - **`openings.ts`:**
      - `SQL_OBJECT_KINDS`, which governs name matching, doors and openings;
      - `OPENING_KINDS`, which makes a file an entry;
      - `sqlKind` and `SQL_DECLARED_KINDS`;
      - for packages, also their members, matching `pkg.member(…)` to the
  package, and tying the body's file to the spec's.
    - **`board.ts`:** `DOOR_KINDS`.
    - **`boardGraph.ts`:** the doors a thing serves.
    - **`estateGraph.ts`:** the join across scans.
    - **The Explorer:** how doors are drawn by kind.
    - **`inspect`:** it prints kinds.
    - **The estate's and the fixtures' docs.**
    - **The oracle's `ADAPTER_KIND`.**

  ### The four reads of a file

  build the symbol index, once with it), each of which tokenized the text twice.
  `readSql` cut that to two. I also saw in the code, without measuring, that
  the C# adapter's `analyze` reads its file twice within one pass (`fileFacts`,

  ## 1. Fence rule: it lost true links, so I stopped and reverted it

  I tried your simple rule: after any quote markers and indentation, a line
  starting with a fence run opens a fence at any depth, and the next such line
  with a run at least as long closes it.

  | Corpus | commonmark.js | Rule before | Simple fence rule |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | This repo (2,005 files) | 51,835 | 51,837 | **51,540** |
  | Dnn.Platform | 2,872 | 2,872 | 2,872 |
  | dbatools | 115 | 115 | 115 |
  | shop | 0 | 0 | 0 |
  | tSQLt | 3 | 3 | 3 |

  **The case:** 295 true links lost, all in
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2025/11/2025-11-14/2025-11-14.md`,
  lines 388 to 432.
  - That passage is a pasted TypeScript compiler error. Each error's `~~~~`
  underline is indented 22 spaces at the top level.
  - CommonMark reads those lines as an indented code block, which ends at the
  next unindented paragraph.
  - The simple rule makes each underline a fence. Line 424 has 67 tildes, and no
  later run is that long (the last underline has 64). So that fence never
  closes and swallows the rest of the file.

  The change is reverted and nothing was committed for item 1; the register
  still holds its one entry.

  An untried idea, for you: open a deep fence only under a list item or in a
  quote. That needs the reader to know whether a line is inside a list item.

  ## 1. A type resolves within what its project can see
  **The rule**, in a new `adapters/dotnetProjects.ts`, used by `csharp.ts`:
  - **Which project a file is in:** the nearest folder above it with a project
  file.
  - **Search order:** the own project, then the projects it references directly,
  then, for SDK-style projects only, what those reference in turn.
  - **Classic and SDK-style differ, as in MSBuild.** A classic project gives the
  compiler only its direct references; the references of a reference are copied
  to the output folder but not compiled against. An SDK-style project passes
  them on transitively unless `DisableTransitiveProjectReferences` is set. A
  `ProjectReference` with `ReferenceOutputAssembly="false"` gives the compiler
  nothing.
  - **Several declarations at one tier** link when they are halves of one
  partial type in one project. Otherwise the name is ambiguous and nothing is
  drawn.
  - **Never linked:** a declaration outside the reach.
  - **Loose files:** a file in no project keeps the whole-workspace reading,
  except that several namesakes draw nothing.
  - **Partial peers** are sought only in the file's own project.
  - **Reflection:** a type named in a string still resolves across the
  workspace, because reflection loads whatever assembly is present.

  **Tests seen failing first**, all in `csharp.test.ts`, five in all:
  - The estate's two `PaymentsController`s, written with the same full name in
  Portal and Gateway, as mojoPortal does.
  - Classic against SDK-style against `DisableTransitiveProjectReferences`.
  - Own project first, partial halves, and true ambiguity, including
  `ReferenceOutputAssembly="false"`.
  - A loose file.
  - The arity case.

  **One regression, caught and mended.** The first build lost 40 of DNN's lines
  that the compiler confirms. All 40 were a file naming `DnnHelper` where the
  project declares both `DnnHelper` and `DnnHelper<TModel>`. Commit `c6b8674b`
  treats one name with different numbers of type parameters as two types, each
  linked as before. The reader still does not match a use's type arguments to an
  arity.

  **The compiler table** (C#-to-C# dependency lines, before at `30edd708` and
  after at `c6b8674b`):

  | | DNN before | DNN after | mojoPortal before | mojoPortal after |

  | Our lines | 20,991 | 20,989 | 8,180 | 6,933 |
  | In both | 20,390 | 20,390 | 6,894 | 6,895 |

  | — of which declared in several files, the compiler bound none | 22 | 20 |
  376 | 18 |
  | The compiler's only | 4,458 | 4,458 | 1,323 | 1,322 |

  **DNN's 30 namesakes did not empty.** They are names like `FileOrder`,

  namespace, in `DotNetNuke.Abstractions`. Our reader takes the own project's

  ## The profile request: lookups indexed once per run
  **What the two functions actually did:**
  - `isWorkspaceNamespace` compared each `using` against every declared
  namespace in the workspace.
  - `toDependencies`' own time was the inlined type lookup. For every name it
  rebuilt the container list and made one map lookup per container and per
  prefix of the chain. That includes the many names that no workspace type ends
  with.
  - The partial-peer loop scanned each peer's member list for every use.

  **The change in `f201fc8f`:**
  - A set of every namespace and of every namespace that contains one.
  - A set of the last segment of every type name, so that a name no type ends
  with is set aside in one lookup.
  - Each scope's containers listed once.
  - A peer's members looked up by name instead of scanned.

  **Counts** (before is project reach, after is `f201fc8f`; the arity change
  alters no count):

  | Files | Type-name lookups before / after | Namespace comparisons before /
  after | Peer member comparisons before / after |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | DNN 400 | 2,259,904 / 52,494 | 205,846 / 2,476 | 42 / 50 |
  | DNN 1,000 | 5,643,504 / 313,006 | 579,886 / 6,194 | 233,984 / 9,212 |
  | DNN 2,000 | 10,333,264 / 860,498 | 1,194,610 / 12,348 | 391,000 / 14,326 |
  | mojoPortal 400 | 2,765,474 / 47,266 | 21,846 / 1,300 | 141,874 / 4,394 |
  | mojoPortal 1,000 | 6,258,518 / 202,288 | 60,752 / 3,052 | 859,400 / 24,762 |
  | mojoPortal 1,900 (all) | 12,616,904 / 626,886 | 133,634 / 5,672 | 4,155,398
  / 92,808 |

  - Namespace comparisons are now one per question, so they grow exactly with
  the files.
  - Type lookups and peer comparisons after the mend still grow faster than N. I
  believe that is the experiment, not the reader: a random draw of N files
  contains both halves of a pair, or a name together with its declaration, more
  often as N grows. So the real work in the sample grows faster than N.

  **No doc changed.**
  - On the measuring script's six runs, the digest of every C# dependency is
  identical before and after.
  - Over the whole corpora, `f201fc8f` regenerated the docs `c6b8674b` had

  ## Fifth leg: handback

  Three commits are on `worktree-agent-ab441e69edee8fd9c`, on top of `1dd746a1`.
  Nothing was amended or pushed, and the working tree is clean.

  **The gate did not pass.** In the last run, one test of my own timed out under
  load (details below), so integration and the audits did not run in it; I ran
  the three audits on their own afterwards.

  1. **`eb1de4b0` Container rule.** Based on your ruling, the writer does not
  model containers.
     - **What triggers it:** a docstring that holds a container. That means the
  format's own list sections (Parameters, Type Parameters, Exceptions, Links,
  Additional Documentation, Unsupported Doc Tags) or an author's own list item
  or block quote.
     - **What it does:** in such a docstring, every line that would open a fence
  or an HTML block of the kinds a closer ends is written behind a backslash,
  closed or not. Those kinds are a comment, `<script>`, `<pre>`, `<style>`,
  `<textarea>`, `<?`, `<!X` and `<![CDATA[`.
     - **What stays:** a docstring with no container keeps the precise rule:
  escape such a line only when nothing later in the docstring closes it.
     - **Lines that only start a new item or quote, or an indented code line:**
  I found none of these that let anything escape. Each ends at the blank line
  the format always writes between a docstring and the next heading, so they
  stay as they are.
     - **Red then green:**
       - A new grammar test writes a Links section where a fence opens in one
  item and a comment opens below the next. The committed writer wrote both bare.
       - The oracle also failed on the committed writer, on `" éB\n<!--\n
  ```"`; a comment ran to line 118.
     - No doc of this repository changed except `document.ts`'s own.

● Noted, and I think it's more than a hint: today is the most evidence we've had
  on it. I'm not proposing anything yet, because two measurements that bear on
  it are still out.

  What Markdown cost us today
  - Names: five rounds of work so that every file and symbol name survives being
    written into a doc and read back. A name like a#b, or a docstring that
    opens a code fence, turns into document structure unless it's escaped.
  - Links: three rounds on reading them. The reader now agrees with the standard
    Markdown parser in all but one place among 51,835 links here. My own rule
    for closing that last gap lost 295 real links and was reverted.
  - Volume: every doc is now read back before it's written, and the largest test
    codebase gets 10,777 doc files (118 MB) written into it.

  Two uses of Markdown are tangled together, and they fare differently
  - Markdown holding generated facts (symbols, dependencies, line numbers).
    Nearly all of the cost above is here. These facts are data derived from
    source, and Markdown is a document format.
  - Markdown holding what people write (the vision, guides, decisions, the
    board). It suits this. Link reading stays with us whatever we do, because a
    document that cites a file is how written words would attach to code.

  Your idea from earlier, a file's own top comment replacing the authored
  section, decides this. If nothing a person wrote lives in the per-file docs,
  they are purely derived, and needn't be Markdown or sit in the mapped
  repository at all. The cost is real too: today you can review docs in a pull
  request and read them on GitHub without the tool, and the history view is
  computed from them.

  Two builders are measuring what the authored sections hold that file comments
  don't, and what it takes today to get from several folders to one World Map. I
  expect both within the hour and will bring you the choice with alternatives
  then.

  Since my last message
  - Engine landing: it has begun. The three branches it waited on are back and
    one builder is joining them.
  - C#: links to a same-named class in the wrong project went from 887 to 0 on
    the second test codebase, with no loss of links the compiler confirms.
  - Oracle PL/SQL: graded against an independent grammar, the reader finds no
    packages. On a package-built codebase about half the expected connections
    are calls into packages.

  Corrections
  - The .NET crash: I said its cause was found. A real overflow was fixed, but
    it isn't shown to be that crash.
  - "A piece is one scan": this was not a new recommendation of mine. You agreed
    it on October 9 (World Map plan (.mdmd/layer-3/world-map.mdmd.md), ticket
    T1) and the engine already merges scans. What's missing is the road for a
    person. Our own board is one scan cut into seven pieces by hand, which is
    what you noticed.
  - Linked server: I now think it needs no new line on the board. Its name can
    be a door the Oracle piece offers, so the wire lands on a door of that name.
    This is being tested.

  - **The other direction:** 36 of the 44 comments are longer than their
  Purpose. A median 69% of a comment's content words never appear in its
  Purpose. What the comments add is usage text, output layout, mechanism detail
  and the owner's motivating quotes; 97 of the 352 comments already carry a date

  - **Worst fits:**

    - The sample programs: their comments speak inside the sample's story
  ("Where stock is kept."), while the Purpose says why the fixture exists in our
  test suite.

  **3. Notes, all 1,093 (`notes.py`)**
  - **What they contain:** 867 are dated; 404 name a record turn; 300 hold
  warnings or rules; 170 name a test; 136 quote the owner; 128 give a trial's or
  decision's state; 88 hold measurements.
  - **Links:** 719 Notes link somewhere. By target:
    - records: 627 docs, 1,688 links
    - design docs: 164 docs
    - other Live Docs: 86 docs
    - pictures: 79 docs, 123 links
    - probes: 79 docs, 102 links
  - The links land on 174 distinct record, probe and picture files.
  - **The existing remedy does not carry them today.** The reverse rule
  (`citingDocuments`, which fills Cited By) only reads documents that discovery
  finds. The config's `exclude` drops `AI-Agent-Workspace/**`, so no record,
  probe or picture README is ever a citer here. Today's Cited By comes from
  `.mdmd/layer-3` (328 entries), `tests/integration` (45), `layer-2` (5),
  `AGENTS.md` (2) and three single sources. `bundleExclude` also hides the
  `*.record.md` day records.
  - The forward rule (`relatedDocsOf`) reads links in the authored words, so it
  has nothing left if they go.
  - For Cited By to carry what Notes carry, three things would have to change:
  the records would have to be discovered for citations; the links would have to
  exist in the reverse direction; and Cited By would have to say why a document
  cites the file (it lists paths only).

  **4. Files with no comment syntax (102)**
  - **Kinds:** 72 JSON (14 of them JSONC tsconfigs that could hold a comment);
  12 png; 4 wav; 3 txt; 2 woff2; one each of jpg, gif, ico, mp4, ttf, woff,
  wasm, patch and nvmrc.
  - **Where:** 53 in `tests/integration/programs`, 23 in fixtures, 5 in
  `packages/engine/grammars`, the rest the repository's own configuration files.
  - **Purposes are short:** median 24 words, at most 68, one sentence each ("The
  surf that loops behind the game.").
  - **Notes run longer:** median 48 words; 56 pass 40. `package.json` holds the
  longest, which is script history.
  - So the "non-plaintext things" are real but few and short.

  **5. What the product reads the authored section for**
  - `document.ts`: writes the default and no-reader blocks, and renders and
  parses them.
  - `generator.ts` prune: keeps a doc whose file is gone if
  `hasMeaningfulAuthoredContent` finds words in it. That would go away.
  - `lint.ts` `validateAuthoredSections` and `RESERVED_HEADING_NAMES`: would go
  away or shrink.
  - `graph.ts`: carries the block verbatim into the graph.
  - `relatedDocsOf`: forward Related Docs (the purple documents, and the 3D
  map's documents when the filter is on).
  - Explorer `card-reading.ts` `purposeOf`: the 2D card's reading line and
  zoomed-out first line, with fixed slots.
  - `detailPanel.ts` and `reader-sections.ts`: Purpose above the generated
  facts, Notes after.
  - `inspect`: related documents both ways, and `--words` at a past moment.
  - History (`summarizeChange`, `summary`, `rows`, `words`, `codec`,
  `textStore`): keeps each commit's authored block by hash, an `unread` state
  for docs before 2026-09-27, and the links from authored words to documents.
  Nothing else in a step depends on authored words. A comment-derived
  description would be better for history, since comments exist at every commit.
  - Explorer search does not read authored words at all.

  **6. What the readers extract**
  - No adapter reads a file-level comment. `SourceAnalysisResult` has no slot
  for a file description, and the doc format has no generated one (only
  per-symbol Summary).
  - TS reads JSDoc per declaration. Python reads class and function docstrings,
  not the module docstring. C# reads `///` on types. PowerShell reads function
  help only, not script help. SQL blanks comments.
  - On the cold run's scratch corpora (dbatools `067b215b`, tSQLt `4a921d0d`,
  DNN `9bc6b06f`), counting a comment as "describing" once licence and banner
  lines are struck:
    - DNN `.cs`: 45 of 3,973. Of the 3,955 leading comments, 3,800 are the .NET
  Foundation licence.
    - tSQLt `.sql`: 7 of 281.
    - DNN `.SqlDataProvider`: at most 139 of 299, mostly one-line change notes.
    - dbatools `.ps1`: 843 of 1,789 have help inside their one function, which
  is already read as that function's summary; only 26 have help at the top.
  - AL-Go and AzureStack-Tools are no longer on disk; I downloaded nothing.

  **7. What would be lost here tomorrow**
  - **Provenance:** 886 docs carry a provenance mark (74 in the Purpose). Of the
  813 such files that have comment syntax, only 325 open with a comment. So
  about 800 files of history and record links would have nowhere to go but a
  comment.
  - **Links:** 642 docs link records, 81 probes, 79 pictures.
  - **Descriptions:** 639 sources with comment syntax have no file-level
  comment, and 102 cannot have one. All 741 have a Purpose today.
  - **Cost of moving descriptions into comments:** about 810 source files
  touched (639 new comments, plus about half of the 352; 21 of 44 sampled
  Purposes say something their comment does not).
  - **Is a comment the right home for a cited summary turn?** Partly, here; many
  comments already quote the owner with a record path. But a comment's link is
  plain text that no reader follows. And for a stranger's repository, no.

  **What I would choose: (ii)**, on three conditions:
  1. Add a generated description slot, filled from each language's conventional
  place: module docstring, `@module` or leading block, `//!`, `// Package`,
  script help, and a SQL header with the banner struck.
  2. Make Cited By count this workspace's records and probes, so that a probe or
  picture README that links a file is shown with it.
  3. Keep today's authored words until they are carried elsewhere; do not delete
  them in the same change.

  The deciding fact: your own C# and T-SQL mostly have no file comment at all,
  so their descriptions will be empty however they are stored. The real question
  is where a person writes them, and (ii) lets that be a document they keep
  rather than a section inside every Live Doc.

  **Not finished**
  - I did not check whether each of the 1,688 record links has a link back from
  the record to the file. That would size how much the reverse rule could carry.
  - The classification has one rater; `classes.tsv` lets a second rater disagree
  claim by claim.
  - Comments outside the sample were only spot-checked by eye.

  ## 1. DNN's 30 lines that still chose a namesake
  I read all 30, against the docs of `f201fc8f`:

  | Cause | Lines |
  | --- | ---: |
  | One name declared both plainly and with type parameters in one project
  (`ISettingsView` / `ISettingsView<TModel>`, `DnnHelper` /
  `DnnHelper<TModel>`). The file uses one; we link both, because the fourth
  leg's arity rule links every arity until a use's type arguments are matched. |
  24 |
  | A `using` written inside the namespace declaration. C# searches it before
  the enclosing namespaces; our reader searches every enclosing namespace first.
  | 4 |
  | A type named in a documentation `cref`. The oracle parses without doc
  comments, so this is a limit of the oracle, not a wrong wire. | 1 |
  | A member name of an anonymous object (`new { BrowserTypes = … }`) read as a
  type name. | 1 |

  **Why the proposed rule does not fit.** In none of the 30 is a candidate's
  namespace invisible to the file.
  - In the four namespace cases, both candidates are visible, and what is wrong
  is the order. Example: `DnnCssInclude.cs` has `namespace
  DotNetNuke.Web.Client.ClientResourceManagement { using
  DotNetNuke.Abstractions.ClientResources; … FileOrder … }`. C# searches that
  declaration's own `using` directives before the outer namespace
  `DotNetNuke.Web.Client`, so it binds the Abstractions `FileOrder`. DNN writes
  every `using` inside the namespace, per StyleCop's SA1200 rule.
  - **Correction:** the order in your brief ("the enclosing namespaces from the
  inside out before the `using`s") is wrong for this layout.

  **C#'s actual order, which is what these cases need:**
  1. For each namespace declaration, from the innermost out: its members, then
  the `using` directives written inside that declaration.
  2. Last, the compilation unit's own `using` directives and the project's
  `global using`s, together with the global namespace.

  **The bigger cause is arity (24 of 30).** The fix is to record how many type
  arguments a use writes (`X` against `X<T>`, and `X<>` in `typeof`) and choose
  the declaration with that arity.

  **Decision for you:** whether to build the declaration-level `using` order and
  the arity matching. Both are plain faults of reading by C#'s own rules.

  ## 2. Compiler edges we lack where only the target declares the name
  40 drawn per corpus with seed 1119, classed and spot-read:

  | Cause | DNN | mojoPortal |
  | --- | ---: | ---: |
  | The type is never written in the file: inferred through `var`, a lambda
  parameter, or a property's or method's return | 31 | 7 |
  | Visible only through a `global using` in another file of the project | 0 |
  29 |
  | Our doc of the target lacks the symbol because `#if`/`#else` splits the
  declaration header. DNN's six are all its copy of log4net (`#if NETCF … class
  PropertiesDictionary … #else … #endif`); mojoPortal's one is unread. | 6 | 1 |
  | A generic argument inside a member access's expression is never visited
  (`EventHandlersContainer<IFriendshipEventHandlers>.Instance`) | 1 | 0 |
  | A property read through `this` that has the type's name
  (`this.UserInfo.UserID`), the same as inferred | 1 | 0 |
  | Written, but its namespace is neither used nor enclosing in the file; not
  read yet | 1 | 3 |

  What I did with each class:
  - **Inferred types:** left. A dependency line today means a type the file
  names.
  - **`global using`:** mended (below).
  - **`#if`/`#else`:** named and left. Mending it means choosing which
  preprocessor symbols are defined, for example a project's `DefineConstants` in
  which configuration, and that is a question for the owner.
  - **Generic argument in an expression:** a plain fault, small; not built yet.

  **The `global using` mend (`9ab320c8`).** C# applies a `global using` to every
  file of its project; our reader applied it only to the file that wrote it.
  - **Test seen failing first:** a `GlobalUsings.cs` in Web opens `AppConfig` to
  Web's other files, and not to a second project.
  - **What changed:** a file's names now also resolve against every `global
  using` in its project, after its own directives.
  - **Unchanged:** the external-namespace dependencies a file lists still come
  only from its own `using`s. `global using static` and global aliases are not
  read, as their non-global forms are not.

  **The compiler table after:**

  | | DNN before | DNN after | mojoPortal before | mojoPortal after |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | Our C#-to-C# lines | 20,989 | 20,989 | 6,933 | 7,060 |
  | In both | 20,390 | 20,390 | 6,895 | 7,022 |
  | Ours only | 599 | 599 | 38 | 38 |
  | The compiler's only | 4,458 | 4,458 | 1,322 | 1,195 |
  | — a type named that only the target declares | 628 | 628 | 172 | 45 |

  mojoPortal gained 127 confirmed lines and no unconfirmed ones. DNN has no
  `global using` and did not move.

  Both items are done. Nothing was built for packages or for the linked server;

  - `afe8dd4e` Read Oracle's CREATE GLOBAL TEMPORARY TABLE and the other words

  `program main`

  The probe README is brought level under "The fourth leg".

  ### 1. The PL/SQL oracle is kept (`027fb095`)
  - **Where it lives.** The emitter is `scripts/oracle/PlSqlEmitter.java`,
  beside the ScriptDom one. The driver and comparison is
  `scripts/oracle/plsql-compare.ts`, run as `npm run oracle:plsql -- <folder>
  [--detail] [--json]`, named the way `oracle:sql` is.
  - **What it fetches.** The ANTLR 4.13.2 tool jar and the grammar's four files,
  into `~/.cache/live-documentation/oracle-plsql`, a 2.5 MB cache of the tool's
  own that is left in place for the next run.
    - The grammar is pinned to grammars-v4 commit
  `de38599628d221099c046e92962f70204d852b54`. That was the last change to
  `sql/plsql` as of November 19, and the files I used: their sizes match.
    - The jar's version and the SHA-256 of every file are written in the script,
  and each fetched file is checked against them.
  - **How it runs.** It generates and compiles the parser in a scratch folder,
  blanks SQL*Plus lines for both readers alike, and prints the comparison.
  Without `java` and `javac` it says so in one line and exits; it installs
  nothing.
  - **What its header says it cannot judge:**
    - It does not resolve names, so calls are graded only when written with two
  or more parts, by a presumption stated in the file.
    - It refuses 98 of utPLSQL's 366 files, and says why: every `create
  noneditionable synonym`, type attributes written `integer default`, `(self as
  …)` in type bodies, and several core package bodies.
    - SQL*Plus lines, `EXEC` lines included, are blanked on both sides.
  - **Reproduced.** Run again on utPLSQL, it gave the third leg's figures
  exactly: 270 of 433 dependency lines right.
  - **Bookkeeping.** `internal-tooling` has a row for it, the two new docs have
  their Purpose and Notes, and both files are in the hand-read list of this
  repository's entry points.

  ### 2. The four tables: a reading fault, mended (`afe8dd4e`)
  - **Why they were missed.** All four are created as `create global temporary
  table`: `ut_coverage_sources_tmp`, `ut_compound_data_tmp`,
  `ut_compound_data_diff_tmp` and `ut_json_data_diff_tmp`. The reader looked for
  `TABLE` right after `CREATE`. A global temporary table is a lasting object of
  its schema; only its rows are temporary.
  - **The mend.** The reader now passes over the words Oracle allows between
  `CREATE [OR REPLACE]` and what it makes:
    - `GLOBAL` / `PRIVATE TEMPORARY`, `SHARDED`, `DUPLICATED`, `IMMUTABLE` and
  `BLOCKCHAIN` tables;
    - `EDITIONABLE` and `NONEDITIONABLE`, which Oracle's own export writes
  before every routine and view;
    - `[NO] FORCE` and `EDITIONING` views.

    T-SQL writes none of these words in that place. An object table (`CREATE
  TABLE t OF type`) and quoted or schema-qualified names were already read
  correctly, and the test now holds them too. The test was red before the mend
  and is green after.
  - **utPLSQL after the mend.** 15 of 15 tables found, all at the right line;
  its other figures are unchanged.
  - **The three T-SQL corpora, re-graded with ScriptDom. Nothing moved:**
    - tSQLt: 1,525 of 1,535 objects, 969 of 969 dependency lines.
    - DNN provider scripts: 4,052 of 4,056 objects, 6,961 of 6,962 dependency
  lines.
    - DNN `.sql`: 51 of 51 objects, 29 of 29 dependency lines.

  ### 3. What the reader records today for a linked server's name
  All three are recorded in the README.
  1. **`OPENQUERY(server, '…')` with the query written out in full.** Each name
  the query uses is recorded as `ORACLE_CENTRAL..CENTRAL.ACCOUNT`: the server
  first, the database empty. It is marked linked and matched by schema and
  object alone. The server name is recorded nowhere else. A computed query is
  not read, and nothing an `OPENQUERY` creates becomes a symbol.
  2. **A four-part name `server.db.schema.object`, or `server..schema.object`.**
  Recorded as written with every part kept, marked linked, and matched by
  schema and object alone; the server and database play no part in the match.
  3. **`EXEC (…) AT server` with the text written out in full.** The text is
  read as running on that server: its uses are written `server..schema.object`,
  as for `OPENQUERY`, and anything it creates is not the script's symbol. `EXEC
  server.db.schema.proc` is simply a four-part name, as in 2.

  This replaces last leg's link-both test.
  - **DNN's own shape:** every `using` inside the namespace, with the outer
  namespace declaring a namesake `FileOrder`.
    - The nearer project's copy must lose when the `using` is inside the
  namespace.
    - It wins when the `using` is outside the namespace.
    - It loses for a file-scoped namespace with the `using` after it.

  `EventHandlersContainer<IFriendshipEventHandlers>.Instance`.

  text;

  On DNN the change gained 20 lines the compiler confirms and lost 11. Both
  kinds of loss are correct:
  - **10 are the oracle's error.** DNN's Groups module declares a user control
  named `List`. The oracle compiles without the framework, so

  dependency.

  anonymous object's member.

  ## 5. Reasons
  `resolveDirectivePath` returns `{ file }` or `{ reason }`. The markup reader
  collects these as `directives.notices`, each `{ reason, subject: "<file>:
  <written path>" }`. The phrases:
  - "paths several projects hold, left unlinked"
  - "paths no file in the workspace holds, left unlinked"
  - "paths two files match without regard to case, left unlinked"
  - "paths from the site's root, left unlinked"

  The last three phrases are my additions beyond yours.

  The C# name search now records whether it met declarations it could not see,
  or stopped at an ambiguity. `toDependencies` returns `{ dependencies, notices
  }` with your two phrases, deduplicated per name per file.

  result:** `notices: directives.notices` in `aspnet.ts`, and `notices:
  resolved.notices` in `csharp.ts`. A `ReaderNotice { reason, subject }` type
  sits in `dotnetPaths.ts` until the channel's own `SourceNotice` replaces it.

● You're right, and I've slowed down. Nothing new is being started: six builders

  Here is the choice on Markdown as the store. Both measurements are in:
  authored sections (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/P
  robes/authored-section/README.md) and several folders to one map (AI-Agent-Wor
  kspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/many-scans/README.md).

  What the several-folders run found. Starting from seven sibling repositories
  and no board, a stranger reaches one World Map in ten commands plus a board
  file written by hand. The tool refused that board four times before accepting
  it. It works only if the board sits in the folder above all the repositories,
  which belongs to no repository. Every repository is left with an untracked
  docs folder, and the first map packs the boxes so that two hide two others.

  Three ways to go

  A. Keep the Markdown doc per file as the store
  What your four work repositories would get: A docs folder written into each
    one, a hand-written board, and Node installed
  What it costs: Mending the ten faults on that road; the escaping work stays
    with us for good
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  B. Keep the facts beside the board; Markdown becomes something you export
  What your four work repositories would get: Nothing written into them. One
    folder, or one HTML file, holds the board and each scan's facts
  What it costs: The vision's first rule changes; the history, the checks and
    the agent skill are re-pointed
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  C. Keep Markdown docs, but write them outside the repositories
  What your four work repositories would get: Nothing written into them
  What it costs: Authored words would sit in a folder nobody commits, so the
    authored section goes anyway, and what remains is A's cost without A's
    benefit

  The rule that changes under B: today "Markdown is canonical"; it would become
  "the source is canonical, the facts are derived from it, and Markdown is one
  way to read them".

  I recommend B, with three things fixed in it:
  - A file's description is its own top comment, read by the tool. For your C#
    and SQL that is usually empty, as it would be under any option.
  - Words people write stay Markdown, as ordinary documents that link to a file
    and are shown with it. In this repository each doc's authored half would
    stay where it is as such a document, moved by script, with nothing rewritten
    by hand.
  - The board stays a readable text file.

  B does not fix two things. Reading links in people's Markdown stays with us,
  and history still needs git, so the command line stays for that.

  B and the single HTML file belong together: the page can only hold a whole map
  if the facts aren't thousands of files. I'd build them after the engine
  landing and the three readers.

  What I don't know yet: whether a browser tab holds a codebase the size of your
  largest, and whether your work browser allows picking a folder. A small test
  page for your work machine would answer both before anything is built.

  From you:
  1. May the per-file Markdown doc stop being the store? Yes, no, or not yet.
  2. Do you want that test page?

  - **What it is:** today's price, plus each drawn outline's height over every

  holds no file in. Counting it would let any outline a bundle passes through
  claim that ground for free.

  parent's, so a column holding only a nested directory is charged for the
  parent, and for the child too if the child holds no card of its own there.
  - **`Cveto`:** a trial is taken only when today's price takes it and C would
  too.

  ## 1. P1 (`1add3544`) and the directory's words
  **P1.** A PowerShell file is a test by its own name only as `Name.Tests.ps1`
  or `Name.Test.ps1`, ignoring case. Other languages keep their name
  conventions. The archetype test and the adapter test both failed first.
  - Files that move from test to implementation: AL-Go 4 (`RunTests.ps1`,
  `RunTests.psm1`, `AnalyzeTests.ps1`, `GetOwnerForE2ETests.ps1`) and tSQLt 4 (3
  build steps and 1 experiment). Nothing else on any corpus, and no doc of this
  repository.
  - The false wire `Actions/RunTests/RunTests.psm1` → `Tests/RunTests.Test.ps1`
  is gone. AL-Go goes from 477 to 476 resolved dependencies, with no other
  change.

  **The directory's words: not built.** I measured your rule (a capital after a
  small letter or a digit begins a word). It moves 64 files from implementation
  to test on the corpora, and none on this repository:

  | Corpus | Folder | Files | Right? |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | AL-Go | `e2eTests/` and beneath | 42 | yes |
  | tSQLt | `TestUtil/`, `TestUtilTests/`, `tSQLtCLR/tSQLtTestUtilCLR/` | 17 |
  yes: tSQLt's support for its own tests |
  | AL-Go | `Actions/RunTests/` | 3 | **no**: the action that runs tests |
  | AL-Go | `Actions/AnalyzeTests/` | 3 | **no**: the action that analyses test
  results |
  | DNN | `Seo.Web/src/components/testUrl/` | 2 | **no**: a UI component that
  tests a URL |

  The rule cannot tell a kind of test (`e2eTests`, `UnitTests`) from something
  done to tests (`RunTests`) or a feature (`testUrl`). It would also call
  `RunTests.psm1` a test again by its folder, which brings back the false wire
  P1 removed. This is the list you asked for in that case.

  ## 2. Pester: what reaches an `It` (`edbaa05c`)
  **Checked against Pester itself.**
  - I saved 3.4.0, 4.10.1 and 5.9.1 with `Save-PSResource -Path` into my private
  folder: 0.4, 2.1 and 1.1 MB, nothing installed.
  - I ran a probe test file under each, in the PowerShell 7.7 here.
  - To get 3.4.0 to load on Linux I edited that private copy once: I removed its
  `Get-WmiObject` entry, which it uses only for a result file's OS line. I also
  set `$env:TEMP`.

  | Assigned or dot-sourced where | 3.4.0 | 4.10.1 | 5.9.1 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | the file's own level | reaches `It` | reaches `It` | **no** |
  | a `Describe`'s or `Context`'s body | yes | yes | **no** |
  | `$script:` in a `Describe`'s body | yes | yes | yes |
  | a `BeforeAll`, `BeforeEach`, or a `Context`'s `BeforeAll` | yes | yes | yes
  |

  A `BeforeAll` sees the file's own level under 3.4.0 and 4.10.1 but not under
  5.9.1. It sees a `Describe` body's variable under none of the three.

  **The rule.** It is written in the adapter and a second time in the oracle's
  emitter (`Test-PesterSetupReaches`, `Test-PesterRunBlockBetween`).
  - A variable assigned once in a `BeforeAll` or `BeforeEach` is followed into
  the `It` blocks of its `Describe` or `Context`, nested ones included.
  - A variable assigned at the file's own level, or in a `Describe` or `Context`
  body, is no longer followed into an `It` or a `BeforeAll`. That case is true
  under 3 and 4 but false under 5, so it is not drawn.
  - The adapter test fails with either half removed.

  **Not done in the rule:**
  - It does not read a file's own statement of its version (`#Requires -Modules
  Pester`, `BeforeDiscovery`, `-ForEach`), so the stricter reading holds for
  every file.
  - I did not probe what `BeforeEach`, `AfterAll` or `AfterEach` see, and the
  narrowing does not touch them.

  **Graded on the corpora.** AL-Go goes from 476 to 492, with none removed;
  dbatools is unchanged.
  - All 16 new wires are tests loading their own action through a `$scriptRoot`
  that a `BeforeAll` assigns. With fewer than 30, the sample is the whole set.
  - I read 3 in source, all true. A second judge (a subagent, blind to my
  verdicts) read all 16: all true under Pester 5 and under 3/4.
  - The oracle, rerun over AL-Go, expects 493 pairs; the adapter draws 492 of
  them and invents none. The one miss is the old written-out-list load in a file
  the grammar still reads with an error.

  |---|---|---|

  **The mends:**
  - A number's multiplier, type suffix and exponent in any case: `1GB`, `10L`,
  `1E3`.
  - A bare loop label after `break` or `continue`: `continue main`.
  - A quoted switch label: `"In Progress" { }`, including `switch -regex`
  patterns. Upstream's own switch tests still pass.
  - `Install-DbaInstance.ps1` and the three functions inside it are no longer
  missing. Its first error was a quoted `-regex` label, so that mend most likely
  freed them, but I did not grade the mends one at a time.

  **Switch as an expression** needed no rule of its own; the small cases read
  without error after the earlier mends.

  **Not mended:**
  - **A member after a method call, as a command argument** (`-Value
  $o.GetType().Name`). Upstream's `_command_element` reads `$o.GetType()` in
  argument mode as a member access followed by a separate argument list. Fixing
  that changes the trees in upstream's own corpus, which the patch does not
  carry. Do we carry upstream corpus changes in the patch?
  - **The typographic quote.** PowerShell 7 here ends a single-quoted string at
  `’`. Whether Windows PowerShell 5.1 does is unverified, and it is marked so in
  the probe for what only Windows can show.

  ## The headline
  Writing the facts to Markdown and reading them back loses nothing. I compared,
  field by field, the graph made straight from the generator's in-memory models
  with the graph read back from the docs it writes (`index.json`). Over 14,336
  files on four workspaces there were 0 differences. The comparison can fail:
  one renamed symbol, one dropped edge and one removed file were all caught.

  So the trial's case is cost, not lost facts. The parser, the value slots' read
  side and the reversible escapes (about 900 of `document.ts`'s 1,253 lines),
  plus about 1,000 lines of tests, exist only so a written doc reads back as its
  model.

  Commit **78feba0e3** on the worktree's branch, on top of a1d580016. No product
  file was changed. The record is a dated probe, marked historical at its top:
  - `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probes/facts-with
  out-docs/README.md` (all seven parts)
  - `probe.cjs`: the comparison, plus an "analysis alone" mode that writes
  nothing. It wraps `renderLiveDoc` in the built CommonJS engine before the
  generator loads.
  - `time-inspect.sh`
  - `results/*.json`: small per-run results.

  The corpora were copied into a private folder, which I deleted afterwards. The
  cold folder was never written to.

  ## Part 2: the comparison
  | Workspace | Files (models / docs) | Differing fields | Only in the docs'
  graph |
  | --- | --- | ---: | --- |
  | This repository | 1,118 / 1,120 | 0 | 2 |
  | dbatools | 1,977 / 1,977 | 0 | 0 |
  | tSQLt | 465 / 465 | 0 | 0 |
  | DNN | 10,776 / 10,776 | 0 | 0 |

  - **The two extra files** (`.vscode/settings.json`, `d3-force-3d.d.ts`) are
  stale docs kept for their authored words. They appear only because my copy
  ignored those files. Runs with `--include` or `--changed` would add more of
  this kind.
  - **No corpus stopped generation.** I removed tSQLt's old five-file exclude
  and every corpus still finished.
  - **The model itself is already shaped by Markdown.** Its links are
  percent-encoded paths relative to where the doc would sit, and the graph
  decodes them again. Dropping the render, read-back and write alone frees
  nothing more; the links would have to become a file plus a symbol.
  - **A bug this hides:** `compose.ts:407` assumes the docs folder sits exactly
  two folders below the workspace.

  ## Part 3: costs
  High load throughout (about 21), so the time differences are rough.

  | Workspace | Full run: s, peak MB | Analysis alone: s, peak MB | Docs written
  | One graph file: pretty / compact / gzipped |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | This repository | 56.1, 485 | 56.1, 417 | 1,120, 4.3 MB | 9.45 / 6.75 / 1.16
  MB |
  | dbatools | 78.3, 492 | 68.7, 488 | 1,977, 4.0 MB, 9.3 MB on disk | 5.6 / 4.2
  / 0.67 MB |
  | tSQLt | 25.3, 310 | 24.6, 305 | 465, 1.2 MB | 2.3 / 1.6 / 0.13 MB |
  | DNN | 250.8, 1,566 | 202.5, 1,161 | 10,776, 22.8 MB text, 61.9 MB on disk |
  60.1 / 39.0 / 3.14 MB |

  - **Deriving the graph from the models** takes 0.03–1.1 s.
  - **`inspect`, one question on DNN:** 10.4–11.8 s today. Deriving the graph
  from the docs is 6.5–7.5 s of that; reading `index.json` instead would be
  1.4–1.9 s. With no stored graph it would mean re-analysing everything, 25–202
  s per question across the corpora.
  - **History:** from the history-rebuild-cost record's own numbers, with no new
  run, the docs' share is about 7–13 % of one commit's time. The cost sits in
  the index pass and the analysis, which removing the docs does not touch.

  ## Where this contradicts the brief or your recommendation
  1. **The count:** a1d580016 has 1,120 docs, not 1,093.
  2. **The skill is wrong:** `reference.md:49` says the command line and the
  Explorer read `index.json`. Both actually re-derive the graph from the docs.
  The only product reader of `index.json` is the history's
  `summarizeChange.ts:97`.
  3. **"Beside the board":** the estate reader already reads each scan's own
  folder (`graphFiles.ts:64–101`). Facts kept beside the board would make one
  scan's facts depend on which board names it, and a scan on two boards would be
  generated twice. I would keep them inside the scan, in a folder that ignores
  itself.
  4. **"Words as ordinary documents that link a file" loses something.** Today
  720 links from authored words to 158 documents count as the file's relations.
  Moved into separate documents, those become document-to-document links, which
  the graph does not carry. The history would also lose them, because its
  sequence carries only the forward links.

     If every doc became a citing document, the bundle would go from 158 to
  1,278 documents. The 3D documentation overlay would gain 1,120 nodes, each
  tied to one file, and Knowledge Sources could not tell a file's own words from
  a design document.
  5. **Without docs the stored graph is a cache.** That reverses the 2026-09-28
  reason "it is not a cache", and should be put to the owner as a reversal.

  ## Forks, with my choice for each
  - **Where the facts live:** inside the scanned folder, in a folder that
  ignores itself. Commit nothing derived.
  - **How a consumer knows they are fresh:** the stored graph carries a
  fingerprint (the generator identity the history already computes, the
  configuration, and a listing of the discovered files). On a mismatch, say
  "stale" and regenerate. The per-file store of the 2026-10-19 decision stays
  the later work; the fingerprint check is what its resolution step would run
  first anyway.
  - **The commands and checks:**
    - `generate` writes the stored graph, and `--check` becomes the fingerprint
  check. Drop `--include` and `--changed` until the per-file store exists.
    - `lint` keeps only its structural checks over the graph.
    - `find-orphans` is deleted.
    - The gate's "Live Docs" stage and CI become "generate, then lint".
    - Pages generates before building.
    - The history's check at the head compares against a fresh generation, and
  should say plainly what it no longer checks.
  - **The export:** a one-way, on-demand Markdown export, never read back. The
  Explorer's download already does exactly this.
  - **A file's own top comment:** one general reader by comment syntax as the
  floor, with each language's own reader overriding it where one exists. In the
  model, call it `description`, not "opening", which already means a route or
  procedure.
  - **What the agent skill reads:** `inspect --json`, plus one new "everything
  about this file" question.
  - **New fork: where a file's own words live.** One words document per file at
  a path convention. In this repository that is the same path, words only, so
  the 8,724 existing links to those paths (from 173 documents) and the history
  keep working. Its links count as the file's.

  ## The road
  Seven steps; each leaves a working product and a green gate.
  1. The generator returns the graph it made, and the history takes it instead
  of reading `index.json`.
  2. Read each file's top comment into the model.
  3. Consumers read the stored graph with its fingerprint.
  4. Move the words into their own documents.
  5. Stop writing per-file docs; deletes the parser and the round-trip tests.
  6. Links in the model become a file plus a symbol.
  7. Add the export command.

  Every step except 7 changes code the history's cache is keyed on. I would land
  1–3 in one rebuild and 4–6 in a second.

  ## Not done
  - No browser; the 1,278-document bundle was counted, not drawn.
  - No phase-by-phase split of the analysis.
  - The cost of the fingerprint check itself (a discovery walk plus `git
  ls-files -s` on DNN) is the next thing I would measure.

  (general-purpose) run in parallel. I checked four of its claims against the
  code and all held.

  The finding. The builder made the connections straight from source, never
  written as Markdown, and held them against what we get today through the docs.
  Across this repository and three real ones (14,336 files) there was no
  difference, and a deliberately planted fault was caught. So Markdown is not
  losing facts. What it costs is:

  - Machinery: about 1,900 lines of code and tests whose only job is reading a
    doc back.
  - Files: on the large .NET repository, 10,776 docs taking 62 MB on disk,
    against one 3 MB file.
  - Time: there, the command line takes 10 to 12 seconds to answer one question;
    about 7 of those are spent re-reading the docs.

  Where I was wrong:

  1. "Facts beside the board." They belong inside each scanned folder, in a
     folder git ignores by itself. Beside the board, the same repository named
     by two boards would be scanned twice and could disagree.
  2. "Written words as loose documents that link a file." Each file should keep
     one words-only document of its own, at the path it has today, written only
     when a person writes one. Loose documents would lose 720 of this
     repository's file-to-document links and add 1,120 extra bodies to the 3D
     map.
  3. The history gains almost nothing. The docs are about a tenth of one
     commit's time there.

  What the change gives up:

  - A commit's diff would no longer show the facts changing beside the code.
  - The stored facts become a cache that must prove it is fresh. On September 28
    we chose the index design because it was not a cache. Today's docs are also
    a derived copy, kept fresh by a gate stage; the new form would be checked
    by every command instead.

  I still recommend going ahead, after the engine and reader landings, in two
  steps:

  1. Change no fact. The docs are still written, so the two ways can be compared
     on every repository.
  2. Move the words and stop writing the docs.

  does. That would be new work, not something the change loses.

● Yes. You've found the simpler system.

  In October you turned down "a doc for a doc": if a document were mapped like a
  file, it would have needed a Live Doc of its own. With the mirror gone, a
  Markdown document can be a file like any other. It gets a reader, its links
  become connections, and its headings become what it offers. In the same
  decision you left open "a document's own links, which linked documentation
  does not read"; this fills that gap.

  What it does to the list I gave you:

  - A note's onward links (the 720): no longer lost. The note is a node, and its
    links to the file and to the decisions log are both drawn.
  - Related documents in the history: no longer lost. A document's links are
    part of each commit's graph like any other connection.
  - Still open: telling a file's own description from a mention, the pointers to
    session records, and repointing this repository's existing links into the
    mirror.

  What it lets us delete. Three special mechanisms fold into the one graph: the
  rule for which documents count as related, the generated Cited By section, and
  the history's separate record of document links.

  Two distinctions I would keep:

  1. A mention is not a dependency. Document links keep their own colour (violet
     today), and "what depends on this file?" must not answer with a README
     unless you ask for mentions.
  2. A document nothing links is not dead code. It is listed as an unlinked
     document, not among unused files.

  Scale here: apart from the 1,093 mirror docs and the session archive (which
  our configuration excludes), this repository tracks 58 Markdown documents.
  Today's bundle already draws 158 documents as bodies in the 3D map, because it
  includes parts of the archive.

  Three things are not settled:

  - Unlinked documents. In October you leaned towards leaving out a document
    that links nothing and is linked by nothing. I would draw it, under a filter
    for documents.
  - The 2D map. How a document looks there needs designing and your eye.
  - The switch. Whether one switch keeps documents out entirely, or only their
    text.

  The numbers to beat, from tonight's survey on the large .NET repository:

  - Size: 60 MB as JSON is written today, 39 MB compact, 3.1 MB gzipped.
  - Speed: 1.4 to 1.9 seconds to parse it before answering one question.
  - In the browser: we have not yet shown that a tab can hold it.

  What I think any format must do here, whatever its speed. Correct me if any of
  these is not a requirement for you:

     installed.

  3. The same facts give the same bytes, so runs can be compared.
