❯ What I think any format must do here, whatever its speed. Correct me if any
  of
    these is not a requirement for you:

    1. The Explorer's page can read it in a browser with no network and nothing
       installed.
    2. Node can read and write it on Windows with nothing compiled on the
  machine.
    3. The same facts give the same bytes, so runs can be compared.
    4. A question can be answered without reading the whole file. That is where
  a
       format like HDF5 could earn its place over JSON.

  Here is what I'll say as a rule of thumb about these lists of requirements in
  general:
  - You are listing off important values that we care about maximizing
  - Reality will likely push back against us and force us to be unable to
  maximize all of our values simultaneously
  - Dimensionality reduction algorithms fit high-dimensional data wildly more
  efficiently by allowing a little bit of nudge in the axes off-orthogonal.

  What you will likely find as you try to maximize the things you care about
  and minimize the ones you don't is that you land on a handful of quite
  radically different solutions with substantial tradeoffs, forcing bends in
  certain rules or constraints to accommodate something that is often even
  better than what we had originally envisioned. Nudge dimensions and
  requirements to permute the idea space and find those actual interesting
  local minima that we would think of as logical-endpoint ideas along various
  logical experiment trajectories.

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Understood, and written into owner.md; running the audits and committing
  before I answer.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● That changes how I'll brief the format research, and I've written your rule
  into owner.md so it carries into later design work.

  I had framed those four points as tests a candidate passes or fails. I'll
  treat them as four values instead: relax each one in turn and follow where it
  leads. The research then brings back a handful of designs that differ in kind,
  each with what it bends, what it buys and the measurements, rather than one
  ranked list of survivors.

  What each bend opens up:

  - Bend "the page reads the store itself": the tools keep one form built for
    questions, and the page is shipped a different form built for drawing.
  - Bend "nothing installed, nothing compiled": a real database engine, with
    queries that never read the whole file.
  - Bend "the same bytes for the same facts": container formats such as HDF5
    that do not write byte-identical files; runs would be compared by content
    instead.
  - Bend "one file": a folder of small pieces fetched as needed. The history's
    words are already stored that way, in 500 parts.

  Where a design bends something you gave as fixed, such as your work machine's
  limits or the page's no-network rule, I'll name the bend so the choice is
  yours.

  The browser cases are at 176 and still running, with no failure in the last
  lines I read.

  └──────┴───────────────┴───────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  The durations in your screenshot include waiting. #275 shows 1 h 18 m, but 76
  minutes of that was queueing behind #274. The workflow runs one at a time and
  keeps only the newest waiting push, which is what you had assumed.

  Pushing less often would save very little. A push that changes no engine code
  costs about two minutes. The minutes are in the full history rebuilds on
  GitHub's machine: 279 of the 882 came from those three runs.

  - #274 was waste. The cache key at the time included package.json, so an
    unrelated change threw the cache away. That was fixed on November 19: the
    history command now decides what can be reused.
  - #276 and #277 were real. Each engine landing that reaches main costs one
    rebuild there, however many commits or pushes carry it. It is the same
    rebuild I ran here tonight, and tonight's landing made it lighter: 50
    minutes instead of two hours.

  What's coming: two or three more engine landings (the readers, then the store
  change in two steps), so about 50 minutes each, once.

  I would change neither the workflow nor the branches. As far as I know GitHub
  doesn't bill standard-runner minutes on a public repository, so the cost is
  the wait and not money; your billing page will confirm that.

  The browser cases are past 590 with 4 failure marks so far. I'll run those
  down when the stage ends, before calling the landing ready.

  First, something I found that you need to know: CI is red on main. Both pushes
  of tonight's landing failed ci.yml on GitHub. The cause is one 3D unit test
  that compared numbers to the last digit, which Node 26 rounds differently from
  Node 22. This was known: the Node trial reported it yesterday and the mend
  was made on the parked 3D branch. I landed Node 26 without carrying it over.
  Our gate here still runs on Node 22, so nothing here showed it.

  - The mend is now on main (0dd9e61d), unpushed. That test file passes under
    both Nodes.
  - Still to check before you push: CI stopped at its unit stage, so the
    integration stage has not run on Node 26 since the landing. A copy of Node
    26 is on this machine from yesterday's trial; I'll run the whole CI check
    under it once the red-case builder is done, and tell you when a push will go
    green.

  Overnight, in this order, with at most two or three builders at once:

  1. Finish the three red browser cases, so a whole run is clean.
  2. History rebuild. GitHub's four-core machine rebuilt all 1,321 changes in 50
     minutes; here, running fourteen at a time, it took 106. Something other
     than the work is costing us, probably disk contention. I'll measure that
     first because it is the cheapest gain.
  3. The deeper history gain, as a measured design and not a landing: keep a
     file's analysis by its contents and reuse it at every commit that doesn't
     change the file. Most files don't change from one commit to the next. It
     would also mean a change to one language's reader re-analyses only that
     language's files. This is the split you endorsed on October 19.
  4. Tests. Measure where the 41-minute browser run and the 5-minute gate spend
     their time, and what stops us running more than two browser workers. Build
     only savings that change no case.
  5. The application. Profile page load and the first picture. The 2D search and
     the 3D settle wait on your choices in the packet.

  The readers' landing follows once the rebuild is quicker, since it needs one.

› Message from general-purpose
  I committed `2c569bfb6` on `worktree-agent-a0dee84f8896c6bcc`, on top of
  `16629d432`. It is marked unfinished and the worktree is clean. Cases 1 and 2
  are mended and green. Case 3 is still red: I fixed two of its three failures,
  but the third has no known cause yet.

  The record is `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-19/Probe
  s/landing-reds/README.md`. It holds every measurement and five pictures, each
  described. It also has a per-case summary with the next step.

  **How I told code from data.** The landing changed the stored doc format, so
  today's builder can't read the old docs. I built the old data with the old
  builder from a `git archive` of `3d2bdc683` into scratch. Then I swapped
  `explorer-data.json` under today's client. All three cases pass on the old
  data and fail on today's: case 1 and case 2 each ran once each way, case 3
  went 8 of 8 green on old and 0 of 8 on new. All three reds come from the map's
  growth, not from the landing's code.

  **Case 1, `local-map-directories.spec.ts` (now line 558).**
  - **What it protects:** freeing the width makes an opened directory stand
  wider, and the link carries that choice to another screen.
  - **Cause:** `scripts/live-docs` gained five files.
    - At the default Width Cost, the shapes priced were no wrap 72,209, 12 rows
  42,023, 6 rows 39,353 (chosen) and 4 rows 42,843.
    - At Width Cost 0 they were 69,049, 37,033, 32,533 (6 rows, chosen again)
  and 34,193.
    - The price's walk stops at the first shape that isn't cheaper, so the lever
  lowers the price but no longer changes the choice. On the old data it went
  from 4 rows to 2.
  - **Is the product right:** yes. It follows the walk's documented rule, and
  today's picture at the default is a sound four-column picture. Whether 2 rows
  would be cheaper today is unknown, because the walk never tries it.
  - **What I changed:** the case now opens the estate sample's `Contracts`, a
  fixed program. It stands narrow at the default (3 columns, 1,002 px) and in 2
  rows at Width Cost 0 (4 columns, 1,374 px).
  - **Proof it can still fail:** I made the shape's price ignore the lever (a
  temporary change, removed and rebuilt). The case went red at `free.shape not
  equal`.
  - **Now:** green 5 of 5.

  **Case 2, `return-cost.spec.ts` (now line 87).**
  - **What it protects:** the Return Cost a link names is the one the search
  prices with, and Tuning shows it.
  - **Cause:** the landing made the manifest and its adapters read through
  `tooling/sourceText.ts`. With that card in the picture, the order with no
  return is cheaper in wire alone. The search adopts it at every Return Cost, 0
  included; on the old data it kept the greedy order at 500 and adopted at
  1,000.
  - **Is the product right:** yes. Today's picture at 500 puts every adapter in
  one column with no return, and it reads well. There is simply no trade left
  there for the lever to decide.
  - **What I changed:** the link's case now uses the Rosetta C# pair
  `Models/ModelFactory.cs` and `Models/Record.cs`. The greedy order shows 2
  returns; the other order shows 1 and costs 1,786 px more wire. A link naming
  500 keeps the greedy order (`[2]`, one trial), a link naming 4,000 adopts the
  other (`[2, 1]`), and Tuning shows each value. The case no longer depends on
  where the default sits.
  - **Proof it can still fail:** I made the search ignore the link's cost. The
  case went red at "at 4,000 px a return is worth the wire".
  - **Now:** green 5 of 5. The two spec files together passed whole, 17 cases.
  The other two cases in this file still use the manifest and pass, but they
  could drift the same way later.

  **Case 3, `force-graph-focus.spec.ts` (now line 182).**
  - **What it protects:** a focused link centres its file, a click on its sphere
  pins it, More opens its details, and Centre on brings it back to the centre.
  - **This contradicts the brief:** on a quiet machine it is not intermittent.
  It failed 8 of 8 at one worker and 10 of 12 at two workers, in three ways:
    - **Turn fails** (1 of 8): the camera turned 0°.
    - **Click misses** (3 of 8): the click did not pin `graph.ts`.
    - **Centre off** (4 of 8): after Centre on, `graph.ts` sat 30 to 46 px from
  the centre.
  - **Cause of the first two, shown:** the page gives a press to the nearest
  sphere or wrapper skin, and a directory shell is drawn as its skin alone. The
  helpers only checked sphere discs. So a turn could start on a shell's skin and
  grab it (camera 0°), and a click could land on the translucent `explorer`
  shell standing in front of `graph.ts`.
  - **What I changed:** a new helper, `pressedAt`, computes what a press would
  take, from the drawn buffers: spheres at their drawn centre and radius, skins
  as their uploaded triangles, skins opening skipped.
    - A background point must take nothing at both ends of the drag and 8 px
  around each.
    - The click point must take `graph.ts` itself.
    - If no point of the frame qualifies, it throws a plain error. There is no
  retry and no longer timeout.
  - **Result after the mend:** 13 runs, with no failure at the turn or the
  click.
  - **Still failing:** every run fails after Centre on (5 of 5 in the last run),
  at line 198. The picture is still (`aria-busy` false, substeps unchanged) and
  the camera stops within half a second. `graph.ts` then rests 14 to 46 px left
  of the room's centre and 2 to 4 px low, steady for six seconds, and the
  offset differs from run to run.
  - **Ruled out:**
    - A turn alone does not cause it: on the old data with one forced turn, 6 of
  6 passed.
    - The detail panel was closed when the framing ran.
  - **Cause: not found**, so I can't yet say whether the page or the case is
  wrong.
  - **Next step:** in a scratch build, log frame by frame after Centre on the
  framing's own `target`, `insets` and the camera's orientation in
  `forceGraphView.ts`. Also repeat the pin and Centre on with the old data, to
  tell the picture from the code.

  **Undone:**
  - `npm run live-docs:generate` ran before I edited the authored notes and
  showed 0 docs changed. I did not run it again after those edits.
  `live-docs:lint` and the markdown link check are clean.
  - `safe:commit` was not run, since I touched nothing outside `tests/e2e`, the
  Live Docs' authored notes and the record. ESLint on the three specs is clean.
  - These are stale but outside my files:
    - the `nominalMeasurer` comment in `directory-flow.ts` about
  `scripts/live-docs`;
    - the decisions log's "`manifest.ts` at 1175".

  **Slip-ups:**
  - My scratchpad already held a folder `old` left by the November 19 round. A
  `git archive … | tar -x` overwrote its `.mdmd` and `.live-docs.config.json`
  with `3d2bdc683`'s. It is temporary and the container rebuild will empty it
  anyway.
  - I ran `git checkout --` on my own edited `force-graph-focus.spec.ts` by
  mistake. It touched only that file, and I reapplied the edit.

  Some refusals happened along the way: the worktree guard refused `git worktree
  add` and compound commands that included git. I split those into separate
  commands; I did not reroute around a refusal.

  No subagent was used.
